#6917. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

#6914. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

While security teams have invested heavily in e-mail protection, endpoint security, and identity controls, new research from Fortra suggests one challenge remains difficult to solve: users.

Research released around the recent Black Hat USA 2026 conference demonstrates that artificial intelligence is increasing the speed and scale of cybersecurity activity without replacing the attack methods defenders already face.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#6913. Buck Mason Just Released a Very Cozy Collab with Sierra Designs

It’s the first team-up between the much-loved menswear brand and iconic outdoor supplier.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

El Niño be damned. Winter is just around the corner, and even in a very weird year for weather, I can guarantee you this: There are cold days ahead. It pays to be prepared for them. And you could do a whole lot worse, preparation-wise, than the new collaboration between Buck Mason and Sierra Designs.

You probably know both brands already, but just in case you don’t, here’s a brief primer. Buck Mason, founded in 2013, makes the kind of cool-but-accessible menswear staples that you can build an entire wardrobe around. Sierra Designs is one of the OG American outdoors brands, founded in 1965 and responsible for iconic designs like the 60/40 mountain parka and all sorts of other equipment and clothing built for exploring nature. Both, handily enough, were born in California—and today they released their first-ever collaboration.

The new collection draws heavily from Sierra Designs’ ‘70s heyday, with reworked versions of an archival down jacket and vest serving as the stars of the show. Both are made from lightweight Japanese nylon and utilize a 600-fill-power, 80/20 blend of down and feathers for insulation. They’re complemented by a range of vintage-y tees, thermals, totes, and caps.

The overall vibe is easygoing and not too techy—exactly the sort of stuff you’ll want to wear in everyday life, not just on some windswept summit. Which is what makes it so damn good. There’s no shortage of high-performance winter gear out there, but much of it falls short on the style front. This is the sort of stuff that’ll do the job but won’t have you looking like you just finished a black-diamond run. Wear it with chinos and hiking boots or jeans and sneakers. To the coffee shop on a blustery morning or the park for a day out with the family. Or, yeah, even in the great outdoors. Just make sure to snag whatever you like soon, as Buck Mason’s collaborations have a nasty habit of selling out.

#6912. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6908. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6907. The Inconvenient Truth About the Electricity AI Requires

AI companies are facing political backlash, but they’re staring down an even bigger problem as they look to take over the world.

He-Man liked to exclaim: “I have the power!” AI should be so lucky.

When it comes to the wielders of artificial intelligence, and the data centers needed to fuel it, language and numbers become inescapably extreme: trillions in investment, gigawatts of electricity, the extinction of the human race. It’s almost cartoonish, until you get down to some brass tacks: The power is not there to fuel these dreams of the would-be Masters of the Universe.

“The U.S. grid is not designed for large data-center loads,” says Anubhav Arora, senior director of global infrastructure at Fitch and a coauthor of a recent paper the ratings agency published on data centers. “Securing reliable, sufficient power is the single greatest bottleneck in data-center development,” the paper’s authors write. (Esquire and Fitch share the same parent company, Hearst.)

In the U.S.—which is by far the world leader in data centers—electricity demand from the facilities could reach up to 649 terawatt-hours (TWh) a year by 2030, according to a 2026 Berkeley Lab analysis, or roughly 12 percent of all U.S. electricity consumption. Powering New York City for an entire year requires one tenth that energy.

Meeting global demand for compute could require nearly $7 trillion in capital investment by 2030, including $5.2 trillion for AI-related data centers, according to McKinsey estimates. Estimates from Goldman Sachs Research say that Europe will need to invest more than $3.5 trillion on its grid in the next ten years to cope with increased demand. (China looks to be fine, but it is slow to the data-center boom.)

And the problem is not just a lack of juice. “The actual grid infrastructure . . . is also basically limited in terms of transmission capacity, and the interconnection process is a little bit outdated,” says Arora. That grid—the network of transmission lines, transformers, and power plants that create and distribute electricity to hundreds of millions of users—is constrained, and the interconnection backlog is massive. Put another way: Not only is the shop running low on milk, but the line to get inside is stretching around the block. “You could build a data center in one and a half to two years, but to get interconnection, it takes five to ten years,” Arora says.

According to Cleanview, a company that tracks U.S. power infrastructure, there are currently 1,454 operating data centers, as well as up to 578 under construction and 2,663 proposed. Companies like Alphabet, Meta, and Anthropic are scrambling. With that scramble, there is money to be made. PJM, the largest power-grid operator in the United States, has been trying to devise a new way to accommodate the data-center boom. Its proposed “Bring Your Own New Capacity” (or BYONC) framework would have allowed new data-center projects to buy energy in the form of a battery and add it to the grid. But in October, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission rejected that portion of PJM’s proposal, sending the grid operator back to the drawing board.

Important here is what kind of data centers we’re talking about. Of the more than 3,200 data centers proposed globally, 800 of them are hyperscalers. These are the biggies. The campuses. Sprawling centers with square footage in the millions and power demands upwards of 100 MW (which could power 100,000 homes). Collectively, some estimates put the global power usage at 200 TWh annually. (That’s juice for 11 million homes.)

How can companies even think about the grid beginning to handle that demand? Increasingly, they aren’t. Many hyperscalers are looking to “bring your own power [BYOP],” says Jonathon Smith, associate director of Sustainable Finance Research at Fitch. In other words, build their own power plants. The problem here is twofold: First, very few people on the planet know how to build something like this. You could buy the biggest army the world has ever seen; try moving it without engineers. Second, it poses very real and very dangerous environmental threats.

Recently, the journal Nature Sustainability published a study revealing that the annual carbon dioxide emissions of the largest data centers in the U.S. are equal to that of a small country, like Paraguay or Denmark. And that study looked at data centers from 2024 and 2025. Data centers have exploded since then, with as much as a 190 percent increase from 2025 to 2026.

When companies like Anthropic say, “Don’t worry, we’re not even touching the grid,” it brings up a cartoon situation more akin to The Simpsons than a Saturday-morning serial. Picture it: a giant data center, clean, cool, not an emission in sight, just humming machines and maybe a security guard. But there’s a pipe going out the back, and if you follow it as it snakes around the corner, it connects to a behemoth power plant, burning enough natural gas to power a city, with plumes of smoke assaulting the sky above. Animated, but closer to the reality of BYOP.

“From an environmental-impact perspective, most of these developers are deploying natural-gas-based assets for power, which release GHG [greenhouse gas] emissions and air pollutants,” says Smith. The cleaner alternatives are renewables (unlikely), nuclear (terrifying), and batteries (no environmental picnic, either).

Among the environmental concerns—which inevitably bleed into the social and economic ones—are these centers’ use of water, which they need in great volume to keep those humming stacks of servers cool. While many experts point to these issues as overblown, especially as data centers look to closed-loop systems (there is an initial dump of water that gets cooled and reused), the reason you won’t stop hearing about water use is because 1) it’s concerning, and 2) water is inherently political and always has been. (Traditionally, we’ve needed the stuff to feed our animals, grow our crops, and, you know, live.) Go back to the early chapters of the Bible and what’s causing strife? Water. Go back even further and some of the first wars ever recorded—4,500 years ago in ancient Sumeria—are over the stuff. And data centers have become a fast-moving political football.

A recent Gallup poll indicates that 71 percent of Americans do not want an AI data center near where they live. Those are some very big numbers. It’s important to clarify that the data centers big tech wants to build near dense populations are for cloud computing, and they make up roughly 70 percent of all data centers. While centers for AI computing and AI training are catching up, these can be built farther away, for the simple reason that we’re happy to wait a few minutes for AI to do our taxes but not for the cloud to recombobulate an email or Instagram post.

That’s still a huge number of data centers that need to be near you and me, so what will we do about it? The politicians are listening. New York State has issued a moratorium. There’s legislation limiting the centers on the table in nine other states. Gina Hinojosa, who just pummeled Greg Abbott in the Governor's race in Texas, campaigned on promises of a moratorium for permitting and construction on new data centers. Norway, the Netherlands, and Denmark are leading the charge on moratoriums and other restrictions in Europe as governments catch up with popular opinion.

Moreover, back in the U.S.A., Donald Trump has touted a “deal” with big tech on data centers, the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, supposedly protecting grids from the biggies. But never has Tip O’Neill’s maxim that “All politics is local” been more salient. More than 500 localities are forming their own moratoriums, zoning freezes, and permit pauses. Even after the midterms, how and if to move forward with data centers is a debate for states, counties, cities, townships, and any other geographic entity that makes laws, collects taxes, and breathes collective air.

As the Democrats take over Congress and data centers become every pol’s favorite new cudgel, as the European Union and member states enact regulatory frameworks (often tied to renewable energy and climate), as elected officials turn away from AI’s once sunnily vague promises, something is becoming clear: It’s humans who have the power.

#6905. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6900. The Newest Apple Watch Is 25% Off for Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Swipe the Series 12 at its lowest price of the year.

It’s no secret that Apple has been dominating tech headlines this year. But between the news of its recent price hikes on MacBooks and iPads and the release of the iPhone Duo (the tech giant’s first foldable), not much fanfare has been made about the new generations of the Apple Watch. As far as we’re concerned, that’s a damn shame, because they’re well worth the upgrade—especially if you can find one on discount.

Right on cue, Amazon’s Black Friday sale is here, and with it comes record-low prices on every Apple Watch, whether you want to shave $100 off the brand-new Apple Watch Series 12, take advantage of a sub-$200 price on the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3, or score the rugged Apple Watch Ultra 4 while it’s under $700.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set to shop. After all, when Apple gadgets are this cheap, they tend to go like hotcakes, so don’t say we didn’t warn you when they inevitably sell out.

Commerce editor Luke Guillory argued that compared to other fitness trackers on the market, Apple was lagging behind with its lack of readiness score functionality—until now. The Apple Watch Series 12 marks the debut of the brand’s very own readiness score thanks to a revamped tracking system. Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made sweeter when it’s on sale for Black Friday.

Given that this brand-new watch dropped to just $350 for October Prime Day, we were expecting more of the same for Black Friday, so this $100 discount, bringing it to just $299, is all the more impressive.

If you’re looking for Apple’s most affordable watch, we suggest turning your attention to the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3. It was actually the watch that converted Gelbart to Apple wearables in the first place, arguing that its beauty is in its simplicity.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This $179 Black Friday deal drops the SE 3 even lower than its previous record-low price of $199, which we first saw during October Prime Day, making the most affordable Apple Watch an even smarter buy.

Our editors have loved every version of the Apple Watch Ultra thus far, and the latest model not only delivers on greatness once again, but also just hit a record-low price for Black Friday.

The Apple Watch Ultra 4 has the same new Readiness features and advanced health tracking the Series 12 has, in addition to the dive functionality, ruggedness specs, and satellite capabilities we’ve come to know and love from the Ultra line. Plus, from a sartorial standpoint, it’s hard to beat the new semi-translucent watch bands, which took design cues from the ocean.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, pick it up. It’s incredibly likely that the deals you see here are the best ones you’ll see for the rest of the year.

#6896. Designers Say This ’70s-Inspired Design Trend Is Making a Glamorous Comeback

Get ready to see this glossy finish everywhere again.

We all know design is reflective. Whether it’s in our personal style, architectural splendor, or cultural ties, great design mirrors the world around us. Now, it’s time for it to mirror itself.

Once dismissed as a tacky, ’70s-era trend (not unlike wood paneling), mirrored surfaces are making a comeback. But don’t worry—today’s mirror craze moves past the floor-to-ceiling panels of the past, opting instead for accents that add light, depth, and dimension.

“I love mirrored surfaces in interiors because they can be so transformative & work in all styles. They can add glitz and glamour, especially when you add beveled details,” says Bridget Tiek of TIEK BYDAY. “I love to see mirrors as a backbar, behind shelves, on doors, ceilings and on walls. Basically everywhere!”

Bolder than traditional ready-to-hang mirrors, mirrored surfaces are often hard to place tastefully. Here’s how designers have brought the striking detail into interiors.

Queer Eye for the Straight Guy star Carson Kressley’s Murray Hill apartment is full of personality, and his sunken living room has several glittering surfaces. The designer embraces mirrored shine as a bold accent, as seen in this disco-ball-tiled end table.

Designer Alexander D. Wilson brought a mirrored backsplash into this Upper East Side apartment to elevate the elegant yet fun palette while creating the illusion of a larger space. NYC apartments often feel like a shoebox. With this classic design trick, they don’t have to look like it.

Interior designer Sarah Vaile brought Manhattan to the Windy City in this punchy coastal home. After coating the walls in Farrow & Ball’s Borrowed Light for a classic Nancy Meyers-esque aesthetic, she framed a nearly ceiling-high mirror in crisp white molding, transforming the panel into a bold decorative piece that amplifies space and cosmopolitan chic.

Bold enough to make a fierce statement in even the smallest piece, interior designer Amy Courtney brought industrial flair to her New York City home with a silvery domed lamp to complement the bold hues of her living room’s palette.

While the glassy, expansive nature of mirrored panels certainly has its charms, if you’re hoping to bring both glossy and textured flair to your design, mirrored tiles are the perfect solution. Designer Shannon Ggem used mirrored tiles in this moody bar design to bring more light to the room without sacrificing the deep-hued palette.

While most rely on millwork to bring style to the walls, designer Michelle Prentice opted for mirror panels on the closet doors of this South Carolina bedroom. Not only do they add depth to the design, but they bring a boutique feel while adding to the visual palette and playing off the centered vanity mirror to unify the wall treatments.

As stainless steel fades from an industry standard to a passing fad, designers are getting creative when it comes to finding chic alternatives. In this monochrome green butler’s pantry, designer Bridget Tiek brought in a glassy paneled door to elevate the appliance design, taking the invisible kitchen trend to new heights.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6895. The 20 Best Colognes to Give as Holiday Gifts

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Impress everyone on your list with these great scents.

Fragrance makes a fantastic holiday gift. It’s something they can wear every day, a good bottle lasts for ages, and it feels undeniably luxurious. The only catch? Dropping serious cash on a scent they don’t actually like would, frankly, suck. Luckily, we’re pretty obsessed with fragrance here at Esquire. We’ve smelled, tested, and worn more colognes than we can count—and we know which ones tend to be crowd-pleasers.

It helps to know what your giftee is into. Do they want something light and fresh they can wear year-round? Something dark and smoky for winter? Or are they new to cologne, meaning a beloved classic is the safest bet? (And we know the fan favorites by heart—as well as all the best new fragrances from the past year.) Whatever their taste (or level of fragrance expertise), we put our heads—and noses—together to find the best colognes to gift everyone on your list.

It’s just sexy. This fragrance is not one to go under the radar and escape people’s attention. From experience, it will draw people in from across the room to ask what you’re wearing. A little clichéd but very much tried and tested. It’s unabashedly smoky and resinous, which makes for a captivating cloud of mystery. —Alfonso Fernández Navas, senior style and market editor

It’s a classic for a reason. Aromatic and bold. I like to wear it for the important kind of evenings when I need an extra bit of refinement. —Mark Burger, social-media editor

Fulton & Roark makes so many delicious fragrances, but Ramble is one of my favorites. It’s inspired by the famous Central Park enclave of the same name, so it’s perfect for any NYC native. The notes of forest air, lavender, and lemon rind create a fresh, woody scent that will transport you to the wilderness. It’s sophisticated and lush, and it works for literally everyone. —Bellamy Richardson, associate commerce editor

A reliable fastball of a men’s going-out scent. Up top, it’s fresh and gets compliments without being an attention-grabbing club citrus fragrance. On the dry down, it’s deep and sophisticated without being too fashion-y and cerebral.

This would be my go-to for most guys. I’ve given bottles to my brother (divorced dad in his 40s) and his oldest son (just turned 21). Gets compliments in both age ranges. —Luke Guillory, commerce editor

A versatile scent that wears well practically any time of the year. The citrusy notes are potent but not overwhelming. You need only two sprays maximum for this to hit. —Eric Francisco, entertainment editor

Snif’s new Double Take cologne is one of our new favorite scents—and at $74, it’s a much more affordable option than some of the more luxury picks on this list. With juicy apricot and fresh orange blossom at the top, creamy sesame milk at the heart, and a classic woody base, it’s beloved by everyone in our office who’s tried it. —B.R.

After years of using Ralph Lauren Polo Gold Blend, my wife took me cologne shopping at Le Labo in the West Village, where we spent more than an hour trying out different fragrances. I ended up going with Baie 19, which brings an earthy and refreshing feeling. It paired well with my body smell, which is a huge factor. Le Labo also offers a huge discount with its refill program; you just have to bring your bottle back. —Mike Kim, digital design director

Woody, rosy, fresh—a bright and sophisticated scent I like to wear all day. Plus, if it’s good enough for Tom Holland, it’s good enough for me. —M.B.

Byredo Mojave Ghost smells effortless, and even my friends who don’t usually like scents always compliment it. The bottle design also makes it perfect to put on display—it looks so chic sitting on a desk or counter! —Emma Saxon, fashion assistant

Ranger Station is a little perfumer out of Nashville, and as such it’s got a lot of country-music references in the lineup. This one, done with the Waylon Jennings estate, embodies the man himself: gruff, masculine, old-fashioned.

It’s a curveball as a gift because it’s heavy on musk, leather, smoke, and spice. It smells like the Stetson Original my grandpa used. (It smells more like it than the modern Stetson Original does, funnily enough.) But that’s why I love the idea. This type of scent has been lost to time, so these days it’s really, really unique. —L.G.

If you know someone who can’t live without coffee, they’ll probably love a coffee-scented cologne. This one from Maison Margiela’s Replica line is one of our favorites thanks to its nice blend of sweet and spicy notes. It also strikes a perfect balance of light and fresh (from top notes of lemon, lavender, and spearmint) and rich gourmand (from notes of warm coffee, milk mousse, and sweet vanilla), so it’s not too heavy in either direction. —B.R.

Pretty much any Dolce & Gabbana cologne is a winner in our books, but The One for Men collection is a good bet for any guy. The eau de parfum is a woody, ambery fragrance centered around orange, sage, and tobacco, giving it a sophisticated overall vibe that works well at the office or on a date. —B.R.

I gravitate toward gifting warm, cozy scents and this one feels perfectly timed for the season. With notes of almond, rose, and vanilla, this powdery gourmand feels soft and intimate. A touch of honey adds some nostalgic sweetness. —Andrea Ríos, fashion assistant

This is a new favorite of mine I’ve been wearing every day. Iris Germanica and vanilla give it a floral sweetness that takes the edge of the manly musk typical of Legend. Plus, it’s hard to go wrong with anything served in a flask. —Bryn Gelbart, associate commerce editor

I really campaigned for this scent during our Grooming Awards earlier this year. It’s insanely wearable. Spice, florals, and citrus top and dry down hit in the warmer months. In the colder months, the warm, earthy heart stands out. It’s soft without being too femme, sturdy without the usual smoke and whiskey smells. I think the difficulty with this fragrance would be finding someone who it doesn’t jive with. —L.G.

Iris Silver Mist by Serge Lutens was first introduced in 1994, and there’s a reason this unisex scent remains so coveted. This fragrance blends the scent of moist, green earth with the light, powdery, and slightly metallic qualities of iris. Let this be your introduction to niche perfumery. —E.S.

If you know anyone who likes warm scents for the colder months, this new release might just be perfect. The notes of cognac and antique wood make me feel as though I’m sitting in an old home next to a roaring fire with a drink in hand. But it’s not overwhelming or cloying—just inviting. —Jonathan Evans, style director

I don’t wear scents on a daily basis, but whenever I spritz one on for a special occasion, it’s always Diptyque. The brand makes the most beautiful yet understated fragrances that are just right, never leaving too heavy of a cast or going unnoticeable. Lazulio is my latest go-to, a more natural scent that is a perfect blend of fresh and classic in a single bottle.

A gift from the brand always stands out thanks to its beautiful packaging. No matter what, the recipient will feel the luxury. Plus, I can bet they don’t have this scent yet. But if you’re not exactly sure what they like, you can play it safe with this discovery set of five scents. —Krista Jones, senior director, commerce and partnerships

One of my favorite scents. Versatile enough to move from day to night yet striking enough to leave an impression no matter the hour. The balance of bergamot, musk, neroli, and vetiver is masterful. The bottle—with tinges of Art Deco influence—is equally sharp. —M.B.

Aside from the grenade casing being so much fun to use, Viktor Rolf’s Spicebomb really booms with its strong combination of pepper and cinnamon. Wears well during the colder seasons and for more formal occasions (despite its aggressive appearance). —E.F.

#6893. Tom Hardy's 5 Most Stylish Characters, Ranked

From Inception's sharply-dressed con-man to the getaway driver in RocknRolla, here’s what you can learn from his on-screen wardrobe.

Tom Hardy doesn’t usually play the good guy. He’s been a forger, a fixer, a getaway driver, a rogue spy, and, in one of his more ambitious films, both halves of the most notorious twins in British crime (not that there’s much competition). Most of them are walking red flags, sure, but they’re red flags in great clothes.

That’s partly down to the costume designers, who’ve given Hardy everything from ’60s East End tailoring to Steve McQueen-inspired ’70s outerwear, but it’s also down to Hardy, who has a particular knack for making a wardrobe feel like his own. Put him in a Western shirt or three-piece and he’ll look equally at home in both (and you’ll want to steal the look, either way).

So, we’ve dug through the archives to rank his best-dressed roles of all time, and one of them’s actually on TV right now. Consider it your new watch list—just ignore the red flags.

Everyone remembers the spinning top and Joseph Gordon-Levitt’s three-piece, but the best-dressed man in Inception is the one who usually gets overlooked: Tom Hardy’s Eames. Costume designer Jeffrey Kurland imagined him as an expat from a Graham Greene novel, and every piece was made from scratch (even though half of it looks straight off a Dries Van Noten runway). Think batik-print shirts, collars spread over relaxed blazers, sharp trousers, and a wallet chain—basically clothes for a guy who spends too long sitting in the sun and definitely doesn’t wear SPF. Then there’s the watch: a gold tank-style antique Kurland picked up from the prop master’s collection—and he’s since forgotten the brand. Reddit’s watch sleuths are still on the case, 16 years and counting.

Legend gave its costume department a very important task: how do you tell Tom Hardy apart from Tom Hardy? Lead designer Caroline Harris and Soho tailor Chris Kerr solved it with buttons: Reggie, the charmer, wears single-breasted suits that feel safe and easy to trust. Ronnie, meanwhile, is the volatile one who gets three-piece double-breasted silhouettes with extra padding to make him look macho. On both sides, though, everything’s cut from vintage fabrics for a lived-in look straight from the hanger. Sixty years on, you could walk into any hotel bar dressed as either and nobody would bat an eye. Until you start talking business, that is.

Inspired by the real-world Cold War scandals that rocked British Intelligence, Tinker Tailor Soldier Spy follows the hunt for a Russian mole inside MI6 in 1973. So, as you might expect, it’s lots of men doing stuff in gray suits. Not Hardy’s Ricki Tarr, though. He’s the only agent with any personality, wardrobe-wise, all thanks to costume designer Jacqueline Durran. Her thinking? Steve McQueen on a mission: a shearling coat in Istanbul, a Baracuta Harrington in Paris, and not a standard suit in sight. The kind of menswear every guy on your FYP is wearing today—Hardy was just 15 years early.

In MobLand, Pierce Brosnan and Helen Mirren head up the Harrigans, an Irish crime family at war on several fronts—and no one knows it better than Hardy. He plays Harry Da Souza, the guy they call when things go wrong, which seems to happen fairly often. Costume designer Rebecca Hale gave him a wardrobe for the job: a battered wax jacket when he’s taking care of the Cotswolds estate, say, or a big overcoat when he’s doing business in the city. There’s one exception to the low-key menswear staples, though: his watch. In season one, he wore a solid gold Rolex GMT-Master II, which is reportedly the most valuable watch in the show. Pretty good for the hired help.

MobLand isn’t Hardy’s first Guy Ritchie gangster part. In RocknRolla, he’s Handsome Bob, the Wild Bunch’s getaway driver. And while everyone else looks like they’d had someone ‘”taken care of” before lunch, Hardy’s low-key menswear staples make him look more like the boy next door. A faded denim shirt. A classic bomber with jeans. A blue checked Western shirt, open just far enough. All simple pieces that let his charm do its thing, which it does when he sweet-talks a lawyer into revealing who’s been grassing to the police. Four years earlier, he played Clarkie in Layer Cake, another member of a London crime crew but one with a very different wardrobe: slicked hair, blazers, open shirts, brandy, and cigars. Similar JD, just with a very different wardrobe.

#6891. Amazon’s Black Friday MacBook Deals Are Pretty Impressive

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Considering the price of RAM, we’ll gladly take $500 off a new MacBook.

If there’s one Apple product line where the recent price hikes hit the hardest, it’s the MacBook. Rising RAM and storage costs have pushed laptop prices up across the board, which makes scoring a new MacBook on sale all the more mission critical.

As luck would have it, Black Friday deals on Apple tech have landed on Amazon, with up to 20 percent off MacBooks—including the brand-new M6 MacBook Pros on sale for the first time ever. Given that these prices have reached record highs lately, these discounts are certainly nothing to sneeze at. In fact, now that the MacBook pricing status quo has been reset, we’d say this is probably as low as MacBook prices will get these days.

Of course, not all MacBooks were created equal. While the standard MacBook Air is great for most, those who are planning on running hardcore software might benefit from a beefed-up MacBook Pro. And if budget is your primary concern, we’re of the opinion that the MacBook Neo is a worthy buy, even if it’s only $50 off right now.

The best MacBook Air deal you’ll find right now is this $300 discount on the 15-inch model. With this laptop, you’ll get Apple’s M5 chip in a fanless, 3.3-pound frame that handles heavy multitasking and light video editing without breaking a sweat. Base storage doubled to 512 GB this year—a great upgrade for storing more apps, photos, and files—and the 15.3-inch Liquid Retina display is sharp and vivid. You also get a 12 MP Center Stage camera that’s great on video calls, Wi-Fi 7 for fast downloads, and up to 18 hours of battery, enough for a busy workday and then some.

If you’d prefer a smaller laptop, 13-inch models are up to 19 percent off. They’ve got everything the 15-inch models have, just in a more portable package. The rule of thumb for choosing between a Pro and an Air? The slimmer Air won’t replace a MacBook Pro for serious creative work or gaming, but for school, business, and general use, it’s the one to get.

Apple 2026 MacBook Air 13-inch (M5, 16 GB RAM, 512 GB)

Apple 2026 MacBook Air 13-Inch (M5, 16 GB RAM, 1 TB)

Apple 2026 MacBook Air 15-inch (M5, 16 GB RAM, 1 TB)

Given that Apple just dropped a new slate of M6 MacBook Pros, in addition to the company’s first-ever touchscreen laptop, we were anticipating discounts on the old range of M5 models to clear out inventory. Sure, the best MacBook Pro deal you’ll find right now is $250 off the 14-inch M5 model—but, if you don’t mind paying just $50 more, you can scoop up the shiny new M6 on sale, complete with improved speed and boosted AI capabilities. It’s extremely rare for Apple to discount its latest laptop so soon after release, and even more rare for it to be $200 off.

Both the M5 and M6 models feature Liquid Retina XDR displays, up to 24 hours of battery, three Thunderbolt 4 ports, HDMI, an SD card slot, and up to 2 TB of storage.

Why go Pro over the Air? The GPU is fast, which makes a difference when editing video, working with high-res photos, or gaming, and the XDR screen is significantly brighter. If your daily workload has started becoming too much for the Air to handle, this is where you should look next. If you want the biggest, most powerful model possible, souped-up MacBook Pros with more RAM are up to $500 off.

Apple 2025 MacBook Pro 14-Inch (M5 Pro, 16 GB RAM, 1 TB)

Apple 2025 MacBook Pro 14-Inch (M5 Pro, 24 GB RAM, 1 TB)

Apple 2025 MacBook Pro 16-Inch (M5 Max, 36 GB RAM, 2 TB)

The MacBook Neo is Apple’s most affordable laptop, even with the recent price hike from $599 to $699. Right now, the Neo is $50 off, sitting smack in the middle between its debut price and post-increase price, at $649—making it far and away the cheapest MacBook.

The Neo is an easy recommendation for anyone looking for their first Mac. It runs on Apple’s A18 Pro chip and is surprisingly quick for browsing, sending email, and streaming video. The 13-inch Liquid Retina display looks great for the price, and the battery lasts a solid 16 hours. It also comes in four fun colors, with the yellow-green citrus being a fan favorite.

There are some trade-offs with going with Apple’s budget model, including storage that starts at 256 GB, just 8 GB of RAM, and no keyboard backlight. You also get only two USB-C ports, so getting a USB-C hub is a must if you plan to connect a lot of accessories. Spending an extra $100 gets you a Neo with 512 GB of storage and a Touch ID sensor for quick unlocking. For students or anyone who mostly sends emails and browses the Web, the Neo is hard to beat.

#6889. If You’re Trying to Dismantle the Smithsonian As We Know It, Maybe Don’t Open the Plan on Your Laptop in Public

In Washington, the mic is always hot, the camera is always on, and the person next to you on the subway has ProPublica’s phone number.

Sometimes you luck into a story. Sometimes you’re in the right place at the right time. Sometimes you’re the person in the newsroom who happens to pick up the phone, or the person standing on the corner when the alarm in the bank goes off. Sometimes you’re Abraham Zapruder. And sometimes you’re riding the Metro in D.C. and somebody opens their laptop next to you and reveals something amazing and/or utterly dreadful.

It is no secret that this administration has had the Smithsonian’s museums in its chicken-greased-fingerprinted sights. It has determined that it was designated by the Almighty to save White People’s History from barbarians of actual historical truth. But how they planned to do it was on the down-low, until an administration apparatchik opened his laptop so that the person sitting next to him could read the planned atrocities, take screenshots of them, and send the screenshots to our friends at ProPublica.

In which we will be transported back to those glorious days for fourth-grade history class.

So who was this indiscreet fellow anyway? In Washington, Sparky, the mic is always hot, the camera is always on, and the person next to you on the subway has ProPublica’s phone number.

The clammy hands of Representative Jim Jordan (R-Van Heusen) should be kept off American history for the same reason that the hands of perverted team physicians are kept off student athletes. Just sayin’.

#6885. Lea Michele Wants a Scream Queens Reboot, Too

The star of Kiehl’s new “Gift Like a Local” campaign also reflects on the legacy of Glee.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

With multiple projects on the horizon, all eyes are on Lea Michele. Glee is getting a reboot; fans are begging for Scream Queens to come back, and the actress and singer is about to embark on a tour with Chess co-star Aaron Tveit. She also just starred in Kiehl’s new “Gift Like a Local” campaign. In partnership with Uber, Michele traveled around to small businesses in New York City and doled out Kiehl’s products. The Glee star even picked most of the stops herself. “Kiehl’s had emailed me and they said, ‘We really want to feature some local businesses in this campaign. Can you send us some of your thoughts?’” she says. “It took me about 30 seconds. I had an email with 50 places.”

It’s a natural collaboration, as the Bronx-born Michele is the perfect shepherd for a curated NYC experience. In many ways, she breathes New York. She’s starred on Broadway multiple times, most recently in Chess, which just received a Grammy nomination for Best Musical Theater Album. In the later seasons of Glee, her character, Rachel Berry, moved to New York to chase her Broadway dreams (Michele is also rumored to be returning to the show’s reboot). She currently lives in New York with her husband and two children. On the morning of our interview, fresh off a sold-out show with Tveit at the Beacon Theater, she’s having a classic New York morning. “I took my kids to school. I was walking in the sunshine, I had my matcha. I’m getting ready for an event right now,” she says. “It just is a great day.”

Below, Michele talks about Glee, her collaboration with Kiehl’s, and whether she’d ever come back for a Scream Queens reboot.

Aaron [Tveit] would love my personal favorite, the Grapefruit Body Cleanser. That’s just my tried-and-true. I used it this morning. If you’re feeling a little tired, it helps you to wake up and feel good. But even if you’re not, if you’re feeling like how I felt this morning, energized and happy, it’s just one of my favorite ways to start the day. Love, love, love their body lotion. It’s one of the only body lotions that I feel like actually works for me and helps to really moisturize my skin. They have all these amazing little kits as well over the holidays. I’ve given my dad Kiehl’s lip balm and eye cream in his stockings every year for the holidays.

I’m really excited to be a part of the Kiehl’s family now. I love our commercial that we shot and the fact that we had the opportunity to support some of the local small businesses.

I grew up here. I was born in the Bronx. My father owned a deli here in the city. We had ice cream parlors and candy shops. We featured Élan Flowers in my neighborhood. Seasons in Times Square is somewhere that I go all the time and buy flowers for people in Broadway shows. I love Onda, it’s an amazing little facial and wellness store in Tribeca. Obviously, so many little coffee shops. I mean, the list is quite long.

I just love everything white. I love all-white. My house is all white, but peonies are my favorite flowers. I love hydrangeas. I love white roses. I just love a big white bouquet of flowers.

[Aaron Tveit] and I just found out this morning that almost the entire tour sold out. [It] took less than 24 hours. At our show last night, I think we learned so much about what the show is and what the show could be. We had to cut 10 songs from the concert because we just have so much material. I don’t think that we’re going to do the same show every night. I think that each city is going to get their own show. We’re going to do some of what I would say are our greatest hits together, and some of our individual hits that I think that I will always do in concert, but I really just feel like there’s so much that we can do.

I want Radio City to be epic. We haven’t really talked about having some friends join us, but that’s also something that we could definitely do if we wanted to and bring out some friends to sing some songs with us. The options are endless.

[Aaron is] one of my dearest friends. His wife is one of my closest friends. He’s friends with my husband. Our children are friends. [It’s] amazing that I get to work with someone that I just respect and care about so much.

It feels awesome. Normally I know when these things are happening, but my publicist texted me in the morning. She was like, “You got a Grammy nomination.” I cried. My reaction was so, even for me, surprising and unexpected. I just broke down. My husband was walking our kids out the door to take them to school, and I was like, “I can’t believe this. We got nominated for a Grammy.” It means so much. I can’t tell you the talent of the cast of Chess. Every single cast member, the most extraordinary talent, our ensemble, the band. To have the cast get this incredible honor, it’s beyond well deserved. I think every single person who saw Chess would agree.

We are constantly one-upping each other in the wellness world. He’ll be like, “Have you seen this? Do you have this?” He had some sort of, I don’t know if it was an infrared mat, and I was like, “Okay, I need that. What’s that?” He saw that I had an amazing water filter in my house, and he’s like, “Okay, what’s that? I need that.” He is probably one of the only people who could rival me in my biohacking, wellness, and taking care of myself.

Most of the time during Chess, if we weren’t talking about Summer House and all of the drama, we were talking about what are all of the wellness things that we’re doing and to try to stay healthy.

I’ve been doing Pilates for years. Emma Roberts took me to my first Pilates class when we were living in New Orleans together, shooting Scream Queens. We went to Romney Studios and did a private lesson. I fell in love with it. Since then, I’ve done mat Pilates and hot Pilates and power Pilates. Everything. I felt pretty good going into this, being like, “I know what I need to know.”

It wasn’t my first time playing a killer on television, which was also kind of funny. It was so awesome to return to television last night after so many years, and I just had a really great time.

I met a little girl last night at the meet-and-greet. She was probably 7 years old, and she came to see the concert with her two dads. Her dads were like, “We just are starting to show her some of the musical numbers.” I was like, “Look at you, you’re a little Rachel Berry.” She had her headband on and her cute little penny loafers, and it was just the sweetest thing. What we all created, and what Ryan and Brad and Ian created with Glee, was just so amazing. The things that people say to me about how much it affected their life and brought them so much joy means the world to me.

I hear stories like that from people all the time, and I know that it really means so much to all of us.

The love for Scream Queens has always been there. Emma [Roberts] and I love that show. We talk about it all the time. Ryan knows, too. Ryan and I talk about it always and I beg him all the time. I’m like, “Can we please bring Scream Queens back?” Ryan should do it. It would be awesome.

It’s so beautiful. I talked a lot about Spring Awakening during my concert last night. We are celebrating 20 years of Spring Awakening opening on Broadway this December. We have a group chat with the original cast. Myself, Lauren Pritchard, and Jonathan Groff are planning a really special private night just for the original cast, a little celebration. I’m so excited that it’s coming to Off-Broadway. My dear, dear, dear sister, Lilli Cooper, is going to be in it. Spring Awakening is such a beautiful and important show, and so anytime that it has the opportunity for people to see it, it should be there. It’s the most beautiful piece of art.

Oh, yeah. I’ve had the opportunity and the real honor to be a part of some incredible original Broadway productions, both with Ragtime and Spring Awakening. It’s an incredible, incredible show.

There are so many. I want to do everything, I really do. I want to do it all. If I am lucky enough to perform on Broadway for the rest of my life, then that would just be the greatest dream. There are so many great shows that have been on Broadway that could be revived and amazing pieces and incredibly talented artists that are writing original pieces as well. So, I’m ready and available for whatever is to come.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6881. 10 Sheer, Minimalist Winter Nail Ideas, From Lavender Cream to Soft Stardust

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

For subtle winter manicure inspiration, look no further.

The allure of soft, understated nails is going nowhere. Rather, throughout 2026, we’ve seen the minimalist manicure movement gain momentum with each new season. “It’s the biggest shift I’ve seen in my chair this year,” says celebrity nail technician Iram Shelton. She explains that the theme has been “natural nails, but better,” which is centered around low-maintenance colors and finishes.

For winter, this translates to sheer, milky tones, short, considered nail shapes and microvariations of wearable nail art. In preparation for party season, expect to see minuscule flecks of metallic gold and star-themed motifs, too. While milky pink nails will always be in style, this winter, it’s all about unexpected sheers like lavender, bronze, and pearly white. Not only do these colors go with everything, but they also are extremely forgiving—you can paint them on yourself without a problem.

Londontown Illuminating Nail Concealer in Bare

Essie Gel Couture Long-Lasting Nail Polish in Liquid Diamonds

Below, find 10 of the most polished, pared-back nail ideas to bookmark for your next appointment.

Last season, we had Hailey Bieber milk nails, followed by Sienna Miller’s ‘Chantilly cream’. Now? Lavender takes the spotlight, in its dreamiest, near-translucent form. A high-shine top coat makes this tone appear even hazier in the light.

Leaning into minimalism doesn’t necessarily mean forgoing color. Take this rich, berry red for proof; when an all-over pigment isn’t on the cards, a sleek micro French tip is equally flattering.

For a simpler take on autumn’s ‘gold fairy dust’ nail trend (as seen on Taylor Swift), draw inspiration from stardust instead. Here, a natural blushed pink base sits beautifully under a gently dazzling, golden shimmer top coat.

Traditional French tips are given a modern, metallic upgrade without veering into futuristic territory. When reserved for the base of the nail, a gold-toned cuff is eye-catching but in a subtle way.

Frosted pearlescent finishes are as luminous as they are delicate—not to mention destined for winter weather. When worn with a cozy cashmere knit, you’d be hard-pressed to find a more perfect pairing.

Square and squoval nails are seeing a revival this season. Keep your shapes cropped, softly rounded, and ultra-glossy if committing to minimalism.

Trade in winter's deep, vibrant mocha, espresso, and cocoa-inspired browns for a sheer bronze pigment. When kept close to the natural nail color, this shade is the best way to embrace elevated color without going overboard.

While the notion of nail art might suggest something elaborate colorful, petite star motifs make a case for the opposite. One on each nail balances a feeling of festivity with practicality for everyday wear.

Granted, a white Christmas might be far on the horizon. But this set sees micro polka dots get a dainty, wintry upgrade, inspired by tiny droplets of snow.

If a micro French isn’t your style, an ombré is worth considering. Begin with a nude beige-pink base color, and watch it blend seamlessly into a cozy, seasonal color.

#6880. Who Is Miley Cyrus’s Husband, Maxx Morando?

The couple officially said “I do” in Palm Springs

Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando are officially married! Nearly one year after their engagement was confirmed, the couple tied the knot on Saturday, November 21, in Palm Springs, California.

The singer, who said “I do” in Saint Laurent, shared black-and-white photos from the wedding on Instagram, simply captioning the post, “M+M 🤍 11.21.26.” In photos, the couple could be seen exchanging vows in a poolside ceremony featuring a stunning display of sweeping white flowers and candles.

In December 2025, Cyrus confirmed her engagement to musician Maxx Morando—a relationship she has largely kept out of the spotlight following her highly publicized marriage to Liam Hemsworth. The Bass Persuades singer was first linked to Morando in November 2021, when the two sparked romance rumors with a joint appearance at the Gucci Love Parade fashion show. (Cyrus later shared a photo of herself with Morando from the event on Instagram.)

Rumors escalated the following month, when Cyrus danced and held hands with Morando in Miami during her New Year’s Eve Party special for NBC. By April 2022, they confirmed their romance during an outing in West Hollywood, where they went all out with the PDA.

Fast forward to the 2024 Grammys and Morando supported Cyrus at the show—where she won her first Grammy for “Flowers”—and he gave her a passionate kiss.

Since then, the two have kept their relationship low-key aside from a few red carpet appearances together. But while Morando has mostly kept his personal life out of the public spotlight, he, like Cyrus, has had a long career in music.

Ahead, everything we know about Cyrus’s new husband, Maxx Morando.

While best known as the drummer for Liily (and for the Regrettes, from 2015 to 2018), Morando also plays guitar. Additionally, he has also collaborated with Cyrus on several of her albums, including her most recent release, Bass Persuades. For the project, he co-produced the album’s title track alongside Cyrus, as well as several others, including “Aftermath” and “REDLIGHTS.” The musician also has songwriting credits, co-writing “Innocent Ways” with Cyrus and being credited on “Aftermath.”

Morando also produced two songs, “Handstand” and “Violet Chemistry,” from the singer’s Endless Summer Vacation LP.

He also collaborated with Cyrus on her 2025 album, Something Beautiful, contributing as both a producer and songwriter on several tracks. Morando also played drums on the record and made a cameo in the “End of the World” music video.

The drummer, born in 1998, is six years Cyrus’s junior. (His birthday also happens to fall exactly a week ahead of the singer’s, on November 16.)

Cyrus opened up about their slight age gap in a 2024 interview with Harper’s Bazaar: “He grew up with a laptop. I had a desktop computer that I shared with my brothers and sisters. … Honestly, he’s raised our dog off Reddit. I’m like, Are you sure we’re supposed to be doing this? And he’s like, On Reddit it says blah, blah, blah.”

Along with his musical endeavors, the drummer has dabbled in fashion design too, with Cyrus as his muse.

Cyrus revealed in September 2021 that Morando contributed cartoon designs to a custom vegan leather set by designer Shane Kastl, which Cyrus wore for a performance that month.

“This look is a one-of-a-kind collaboration between two of my favorite emerging artists, Maxx Morando and Shane Kastl,” Cyrus said of the special look in an interview. “Their collaboration is the perfect example of sustainability becoming the next fashion phase, and it proves that anything can be reinvented, which resonates with me deeply.”

In an interview for Bazaar’s December 2024 cover story, Cyrus opened up about her relationship with Morando, saying, “He’s very similar to me. We just don’t take life too seriously.”

She also shared how working with Morando on musical projects helped them bond: “I worked with my dad forever. That’s how me and my ex-husband met each other. I’ve always worked with the people that I love,” Cyrus says. “And Maxx just inspires me so much.”

The couple’s engagement was confirmed in December 2025, after Cyrus sparked rumors by wearing a diamond sparkler on her left-hand ring finger at the premiere of Avatar: Fire and Ash in LA.

Morando keeps an extremely private, low profile in both his personal and professional life. While the musician does have an Instagram, he rarely posts, aside from sharing new music that he works on with both his band and Cyrus.

In March 2023, a source told People that Morando is a “cool guy” who is drama-free, a trait that Cyrus finds extremely attractive.

“He has no interest in being a huge celebrity; he’s very low-key. Miley loves these qualities,” the source said. “Miley can be herself. They are supportive of each other’s careers. Miley is enjoying life and things are great.”

Following a year-long engagement, the Morando and Cyrus officially wed in an intimate ceremony held in Palm Springs, California. The couple shared photos from the wedding on Instagram, which included black-and-white portraits of the couple with their dog, as well as a sweet photo of them kissing after saying “I do.”

For the ceremony, the bride wore a long-sleeve, backless Saint Laurent wedding gown, while Morando kept things classic in a black suit.

The poolside ceremony took place at Frank Sinatra’s former home, which was transformed with hundreds of white roses for the occasion. In photos obtained by DeuxMoi, the couple could be seen exchanging vows by the pool, in front of an intimate crowd of guests dressed in all-black attire.

#6879. This Forgotten ’90s Comedy Is the Most Demented Thanksgiving Movie Ever

Ed O’Neill and Ethan Embry go on the road in Dutch but end up in some bizarre and disturbing places.

Let’s start off by acknowledging that the late John Hughes wrote and directed the greatest of all Thanksgiving movies with Planes, Trains and Automobiles. There are definitely other great ones, but … Dutch is a different kind of creature. This is a Thanksgiving family comedy in which a grown man fights, kidnaps, and abandons a child. Occasionally he holds him at gunpoint. It’s a rotten child but a kid nonetheless. The movie still made me laugh, but it was mostly in disbelief.

This road-trip saga that Hughes penned and produced in 1991 is by the numbers in most ways but excels in its brazen wrongness, even by the free-range standards of Generation X. I come here to praise this messy movie, not to bury it. It’s a relic from when films could be wilder, shaggier, and more shocking than the rigidly tame family comedies of today. Dutch is many things, but it is not refined to baby-food levels of inoffensive smoothness.

Hughes didn’t direct this one, handing off those duties to Peter Faiman, who had worked extensively in Australian TV and only made one other feature film: 1986’s Crocodile Dundee. I can’t help but feel this was an experiment gone wrong, or maybe a first draft that needed another pass from Hughes. It’s built out of leftovers from better road-trip movies, and like any Frankenstein’s monster, this one runs straight into a wall and ultimately falls apart.

But if you want something different for your Thanksgiving watch, go find this one. Fair warning: It’s not streaming anywhere in the United States, so you’ll have to seek it out on a sketchy Russian bootleg site that makes it look like the footage was ripped from a worn-down VHS.

Dutch stars Ed O’Neill as the title character, a blue-collar guy sent to pick up his girlfriend’s blue-blooded son from a Georgia boarding school and deliver him to Chicago in time for the holiday feast. The not-so-surprising catch is that the kid (played by a 13-year-old Ethan Embry) mercilessly despises Dutch, and honestly the feeling is mutual.

Doyle is one of the most hateful kids in comedy history. Even the ginger demon of Problem Child was more cartoonishly mischievous than sociopathic. Embry, who was then credited as Ethan Randall, isn’t playing a troublemaking scamp like the kid in O. Henry’s “The Ransom of Red Chief” or Hughes’s own Home Alone. He’s a smug sociopath with a violent mean streak.

We learn that Dutch is a salt-of-the-earth cousin to O’Neill’s uncouth Al Bundy from Married with Children because he flicks the caviar off his cracker at a fancy party. (“I make it a rule never to eat anything a fish deposits in a riverbed.”) Okay, fair.

Compare that to how we meet Doyle: Another young boy in his boarding school comes to his room to say he has a call on the dormitory’s community phone line. “Someone from any sort of decent background would knock,” Embry’s character hisses. He then corrects the kid’s vocabulary and threatens to report him to the school administrators for mild cursing. So he’s a snob, a prick, and a rat. But even this is within the bounds of film comedy. You could picture an older version of him as a stuck-up villain in Caddyshack or Animal House.

Then there’s another, even more disturbing phone scene when Doyle gets the bad news that his rich father won’t be helping him get out of this road trip with Dutch. He hangs up and stalks past an elderly janitor who is sweeping the hallway, silently kicking over the garbage can to scatter trash across the helpless old man’s feet. There’s no joke. He’s just a son of a bitch.

Doyle would fit in great with the Children of the Corn, except he’d scowl at their shabby farm clothes.

Obviously this kid is going to get some form of comeuppance. When Dutch shows up at his dorm room to start the trip, Doyle beats him with a golf club and shoots him in the testicles with a BB gun. Something about this is much more violent and visceral than the Looney Tunes–style pratfalls and clobberings of Home Alone.

They have a fistfight in the tiny room, and Dutch seizes the weapon. Does he toss it away or break it? No—he uses it himself to hold the kid at gunpoint. We have just crossed beyond the boundaries of comedy, never to return. Dutch then ties the boy up, lashes him to a hockey stick for easier carrying, and stuffs underwear in his mouth to muffle his cries.

You know a movie is going off the rails when watching it makes you feel like you should be testifying against the characters as a witness for the prosecution.

So begins the drive, with Dutch and Doyle doing their best to annoy each other verbally. Then comes a bizarre scene in which Dutch stops for fireworks for some reason and sets them off in a snowy field while the kid watches from the car. I suppose he’s trying to inspire a little playfulness in the boy, but it just seems like Dutch has lost it and is indulging a pyromaniac streak.

It goes wrong and he sets his coat on fire, which makes it harder to understand why he loses his temper when the boy flicks the butt of his lit cigar at him. The man just went mano a mano with a whizzing fireworks spinner, but this is what lights his fuse?

Dutch does the reasonable thing. He abandons the boy in the snowy night and tells him to walk the rest of the way to the motel. “About 50 miles down the road!” Fifty miles? Okay, Dutch, I believe we’ve added another felony to the charges.

This isn’t one of those moments where the grown-up pulls over just beyond the tree line and waits for him. Dutch actually goes to the hotel, although he occasionally puts down his paperback and checks the window to see if the boy has made it there alive. When Doyle finally does show up, shivering and wet, Dutch says, “Here’s something you should know about me. I don’t fool around. You piss me off, I react.”

Uncle Buck certainly had its off-color and irresponsible moments, but even that havoc-prone babysitter might have called Child Protective Services over this situation.

Doyle responds by stealing Dutch’s car keys while he sleeps and parking the sedan sideways in the snowy road, where it is slammed into by an 18-wheeler. This is a comedy, so the hapless truck driver is not killed on impact.

Faiman shoots all of this with too much gritty realism. Maybe it would work if it was all a little zanier and over the top (think of Vacation), but Dutch just plays less like a series of set pieces and more like an array of crime scenes. They don’t call anyone for help because Dutch doesn’t want to admit to his girlfriend how badly things are going, so they hitchhike the rest of the way and stay in a homeless shelter, where the rotten kid learns that a lot of people have it much worse than he does.

At one point, they are picked up by a pair of pretty young women who are driving home from a job at the Superdome in New Orleans. “We’re call girls,” one of them explains. Hoo boy. Here we go.

But thankfully—mercifully—Dutch goes no further than showing the kid sleeping in the backseat with his head resting on the chest of one of the women. Not great, but it could be worse. (Worse is when Dutch tries to bond with the child over a deck of naked playing cards.)

Even Dutch seems to recognize that “prostitute road trip” probably isn’t the ideal situation for a young boy. But he explains it in the most vulgar way possible: “You and I are riding in the backseat from now on, because what that missile twister can teach you, you don’t need to learn until you’re in prison.” Then the ladies drive off without them, taking Dutch’s wallet and leaving the pair stranded again.

They spend the next leg of their trip riding in the open concrete pipes in the back of a long-haul truck, but they fall asleep and wake up in a trailer depot where they are roughed up by two security guards. Doyle responds by drawing his BB gun on them and making them believe he is going to blow their heads off.

This same joke was deployed by Chevy Chase in the Hughes-scripted National Lampoon’s Vacation (“Are you kidding, this is a Magnum P.I.”) but here, again, it just plays as psychotic violence rather than absurd humor. “The kid is crazy,” Dutch tells the men as they beg for their lives.

By the time the duo finally get home for the Thanksgiving meal, the hostility between Dutch and the kid has softened into a grudging respect. I know: Surprise! Back at the mother’s house, Dutch physically assaults the boy’s callous father, leaving a mark with his pinkie ring on the jerk’s forehead. Instead of an assault charge, it all ends happily ever after with a callback to an earlier gag: Dutch pulls the BB pistol from his pocket and says it’s time to get the revenge he promised he would one day seek. As the boy’s smile fades, Dutch aims the gun at him—and us (!!!)—as the film cuts to the credits.

Oh, Dutch … you dangerously inappropriate maniac. Don’t ever change. And, well, he doesn’t, although the kid goes through the expected metamorphosis to become a more sensitive and understanding human.

They don’t make ’em like this anymore, as the saying goes, and I actually think that’s a pity. Dutch isn’t all that funny, but neither is toothless humor that’s overly safe. I’d gladly tolerate a few misfires like this if it meant more truly unhinged comedies like we had in the ’80s, ’90s, and early 2000s. Dutch stands as a throwback to when small-scale comedies weren’t afraid to take big swings—even if they occasionally landed against someone’s skull or testicles.
