#6846. Of Course Trump Found a Way to Plug His Big, Ugly Ballroom When Partaking in a Beloved Thanksgiving Tradition

The idea of turkeys in that Gilded Palace of Sin is just too damn on the nose.

(Alternative Musical Accompaniment to the Last Post of the Week from the Blog’s Favourite Living Canadian)

We’re battening down here for the predicted Thanksgiving Gale. (The famous Perfect Storm is known as the Halloween Gale. We name storms like that. We are fcking adorable that way.) Apparently, this mother is going to slam the entire northeast corridor from Washington to Maine. Which means massive chaos at a number of very busy airports. And given the performance of the secretary of transportation and the secretary of homeland security when a squirrel recently got into the wiring in El Dorado, Arkansas, I have never been more glad not to be traveling this weekend. At least we will be spared another goony-bird press conference from Sean Duffy and Markwayne Mullin, referred to by writer and musician David Ferguson immortally as Dumb and Plumber. That’s a keeper.

It’s going to be tough to fly, tougher to take a train, and even tougher than that to drive. And the storm is going to lock people indoors with their obnoxious relatives, which can only lead to alcoholic embarrassments and domestic mayhem. Of course, it always could be worse. We’re all being stuck in the house by the elements of the natural world, whereas the folks in Iowa and all over the Midwest are being gifted with a cornucopia of toxic clouds for the holiday. From The New York Times:

What’s worse? Being stuck in the house with Meemaw, who found the bourbon, or being stuck in the family van, heading south, with Meemaw, who found the bourbon? The Army’s got your back, Iowa, but pack a bag and scout out a route to Honduras anyway. Wow.

And I’m sure this last-minute FEMA warning didn’t confuse anyone.

Don’t just do something, dammit, stand there.

And not for nothing, but the Army’s been acting sketchy about this whole business almost from jump. From The Washington Post:

Did the president use the charming tradition of pardoning a turkey for Thanksgiving as an excuse for plugging his Gilded Palace of Sin? (Thanks, Gram.) What do you think? From Axios:

Turkeys in that ballroom are just too damn on the nose.

The 2026 midterm elections continue to bless us. Representative Scott DesJarlais, perhaps the worst human ever elected to the House of Representatives, is gone baby gone, beaten by Democratic candidate Victoria Broderick. DesJarlais’s rap sheet is nigh unprecedented. This all came out in 2012, and the voters kept sending this guy back to Washington by fat margins until two weeks ago. He lost to Broderick, a proudly pansexual 34-year-old mother of two whom he beat handily in 2024. However, since then, the Republican Tennessee legislature brilliantly redrew the maps, adding slices of Nashville to the Fourth Congressional District, a change that benefitted Broderick. So not only is Congress liberated from the likes of DesJarlais, but Broderick’s win was also another example of how Republican ratfcking of the redistricting process bit them on the ass. Heh, heh, heh.

Weekly WWOZ Pick to Click: “Happy Days” (McCoy Tyner): Yeah, I pretty much still love New Orleans.

Weekly Visit to the Pathé Archives: Here, from 1940, is FDR celebrating Thanksgiving with the patients at the Warm Springs polio facility where he once was a patient. The narrator makes a funny about the U.S. joining “the war” against polio. FDR chats up a young patient who is chowing down on a turkey leg that is as big as her head. It looks like it was fun to have a real president.

Discovery Corner: Hey, look what we found! From Live Science:

I have to say, a “floating castle” sounds like a terrible idea, and wearing chain mail on a floating castle sounds like an even worse one. If the floating castle blows up and sinks, then it’s fairly hard to swim in chain mail.

Old King Hans has come down as a jolly old soul, but he was quite the political schemer. He also died in one of those prosaic ways kings died in those days. He fell from his horse.

Hey, SciNews, is it a good day for dinosaur news? It’s always a good day for dinosaur news!

Once again, I point out that paleontology doesn’t necessarily require sweating over some limestone cliff face. Grab a sandwich and a soda and hit the drawers of the file cabinets in museum basements. Surprises like this make me happy they lived then.

In honor of the holiday, I will not close with the usual recitation of the nation’s woes. But do spare a thought for the people—and the cows, and the sheep—under the plume in the Midwest. Make sure, however, to be grateful for all those things that warm your heart and make all your lights shine. And all say amen.

Why the Hell Was Jensen Huang Reinstated?

#6842. 13 Menswear Sales Worth Shopping on Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Here’s how to save big on Lacoste polos, Filson jackets, and designer menswear.

If you’re looking hard enough for sales on menswear, you can almost always find a discount on something decent, a pair of shoes, a shirt maybe, but you have to scrounge. Every other week of the year, that’s just how it is. But this week, the week of Black Friday, the opposite is true. Every store and their mother is having a sale and suddenly, it’s not easy to tell which markdowns are gonna go fast and which ones will still be there when you log on again in December. Luckily, you have me. Year-round, it’s my job to keep tabs on deals so I know which ones to pounce on this Black Friday.

On Amazon, there’s my favorite Lacoste polo, which is actually on an outrageous sale going up to 40 percent off if you’re the right size. Filson jackets are 25 percent off. Lululemon isn’t just for the gals, it has a ton of great stuff for men and its Black Friday sale is absolutely stacked. And REI is doing its classic “We hate Black Friday but are doing a sale anyway” thing, and as always we are here for it.

Let’s start with the personal favorites; after that we’ll just list deals alphabetically by brand. First, Amazon. Yeah, yeah, everything is Amazon now. But when Bezos is offering big discounts on Lacoste polos and Levi’s, we listen.

Nothing is ever going to beat the cool of a beat-to-hell Filson jacket. If you’ve been holding off on buying one, I can’t recommend it enough. Plus, Black Friday is the perfect time to get one for 25 percent off.

I tell regular—meaning normal guys, not fashion-y dorks like me—that Huckberry is really the answer to 90 percent of what they want. All of a man’s casual wardrobe can be bought here; the only thing it doesn’t stock well is suits and tailoring. I’m seeing up to 40 percent off on seasonal shirts, and up to 30 percent off jackets, sweaters, and pants; that’s as good as it gets, folks.

Flint and Tinder Bolinas Johnny Collar Sweater Polo

Flint and Tinder Boiled Wool Chore Blazer

There’s nowhere better for well-made classics of American menswear. It’s one of the brands I come back to time and time again for sweaters, shirts, anything really. This holiday, J. Press has 40 percent off elevated basics that will last.

J. Press “Ivy Guy” Blue Striped Oxford Cloth Sport Shirt

J. Press Made-in-England Khaki Drywax Cotton Overshirt

Any time Lululemon does a discount is noteworthy. The brand is averse to any seasonal sales, so Black Friday is your best bet here. Through Cyber Monday, you can get big discounts (I’m talking 40-plus percent off) on the usual best sellers—just note that for the most part, only certain colors are discounted.

Lululemon Smooth Spacer Classic-Fit Pullover Hoodie

If you’re not a regular Huckberry guy but a man who considers himself “into fashion” or at least “stylish,” you ought to be hitting the Mr Porter sale. The sale is absolutely massive—over 12,000 items are up to 50 percent off—so I’d go in with a plan. Have one item, or even one brand, you want to check out; do that and that only. My hunt is for a really good jacket—sport coat, blazer, suit separate, or whatever you want to call it. That’s what I’m showing, but you need to do some of your own hunting on this one.

Brunello Cucinelli Shearling-Trimmed Suede Trucker Jacket

Maison Margiela Replica Suede-Trimmed Leather Sneakers

All right, the rest of these I’m presenting brand by brand in alphabetical order. I’ll spare you a spiel on each one, but if you need some guidance, let me call out a few heavy hitters.

Del Toro, which has for a long time just been a really good rich guy made-in-Italy shoe brand, has started putting out some really lovely elevated casual clothing—all vintage Italian-inspired stuff—and a majority of it is 20 percent off. And J. Crew has its usual sale going on, but an extra 40 percent off sale styles thrown in with the code FRIDAY on top of that. Todd Snyder is giving 30 percent off a ton of items with the code BLACKFRIDAY. And of course, you can’t go wrong at perennial favorite outlets REI and Nordstrom—both are featuring affordable menswear at a discount for Black Friday.

#6835. 14 Design Within Reach Finds Designers Say Are Worth Buying for Your Home

They’ve used these items in projects and even in their own spaces.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The best designers know when and where to shop at most home decor stores. For example, IKEA suits tight budgets and DIY-friendly spaces, while Anthropologie is perfect for a whiff of whimsy. However, if you want to treat yourself to some sleek, splurge-worthy pieces, consider this your sign to mosey over to Design Within Reach, or DWR.

Since its founding in 1998, DWR has been the ultimate resource for buying midcentury and modern pieces. Though its physical and digital shelves are stocked with iconic contemporary furniture like the Eames Lounge Chair and Eero Saarinen’s Tulip Table, it’s possible to pick up some great wares without the astronomical sticker shock.

To help find high design for a great value, we asked several designers about the DWR items they reach for time and time again. Best of all? Everything’s under $1,000, so you won’t have to completely break the bank to treat yourself.

“One of my favorite things to purchase from DWR—whether as a gift for a client, friend, or a treat for myself—is Flamingo Estate’s candles. My go-to scents are the Tuscan Rosemary in the fall, Agrigento Olive Tree for any time of year, and Heirloom Tomato for any of my gardening friends and family. Beautifully packaged and incredibly clean-burning, these are a must!”—Darci Hether, founder of Darci Hether New York

“We recently used the Fin bedside tables in a client’s home in Sag Harbor. I love how the pill-shaped silhouette softens the whole bedside setup. There are so many straight lines in a bedroom, and that one curve on either side of the bed gave the room a little personality while keeping it calm. I could see them working just as easily in a more tailored city bedroom as they do in a relaxed Hamptons home.” —Sarah Tract of Sarah Tract Interiors

“We love to incorporate the Seine Portable Lamp into our designs for its beautiful balance of form and function. The textured glass casts a soft, ambient glow, while the antique brass detailing adds a timeless, sophisticated touch. Its cordless design makes it incredibly versatile, perfect for creating an intimate atmosphere on a dining table, bookshelf, or anywhere a little extra warmth is needed. It's one of those small details that makes a space feel thoughtfully layered and inviting.”—Lindsey Christie, owner and principal designer of Lindsey Grace Interiors

"We love to source outdoor chairs from DWR. The Palissade Side Chair is a favorite: It’s a great value, a true workhorse, and its modern lines and colors look just as good indoors as they do on a terrace."—Melissa Hall Simmons, principal of Studio MH

“I’m all about a well-designed dog bed. They take up a surprising amount of space, yet they’re often overlooked when designing a room. This one feels considered enough to fit seamlessly into the space while giving your dog a comfortable place of their own.”—Julia Newman, founder of Julia Adele Design

“The Eames Hang-It-All by Herman Miller is especially great in a guest bedroom to give guests a place to hang a robe, a tote bag, or anything they'd like to keep off the floor.”—Christine Varela, architect at ODE Design

The versatility of colors, plus size and height variation of the knobs, makes it a widely adaptable piece for all different types of spaces. I’ll even cluster two to three together in a row for a larger space.”—Jenny Williamson, founder and chief creative officer of West Rose Design

“The Drum Pouf is incredibly versatile—with the added Drum Pouf Tray—both to put your feet up on and use as a coffee table. I love to use them in multiples, and they are lightweight and can be moved to suit your space.” —Linda Eyles of Linda Eyles Design

“DWR’s Story Bookcase is a go-to item. At only 15 inches wide and eight inches deep, it allows you to fit book storage just about anywhere! #6834. Geri Halliwell’s New “Dark Honey” Hair Is Winter’s Most Expensive-Looking Color Trend: How to Recreate It

Rich, warm, and glossy, the Spice Girl’s new shade is winter-ready

Few hair colors are as iconic and instantly nostalgic as Geri Halliwell's natural red. The hue, synonymous with her nickname “Ginger Spice,” has, of course, evolved over the years, from copper highlights woven through fiery red lengths to a full head of golden blond and lighter strawberry-blond tones.

For winter, Halliwell has swapped her signature brighter tones for a richer shade. Enter dark honey hair. "What I absolutely love about Geri’s dark honey hair is the richness and depth of the color. A beautiful chestnut base with ribbons of warm honey, caramel, and subtle amber runs through the lengths and ends. It’s a wonderful evolution of her signature warmth, but with a softer, more understated finish," says celebrity hairstylist and Remi Cachet expert Adam Reed.

At the premiere of Only Murders in London, Halliwell wore her fresh hair color in a soft side part with dressed-out waves, a combination Reed says helps different tones catch the light.

The rich, luminous color adds subtle dimension that doesn't focus on dramatic placement, but on healthy-looking hair. "To recreate the look, I’d ask your colorist to maintain that beautiful brunette depth through the roots, introducing delicately placed honey and caramel lights through the mid-lengths and ends. A personalized gloss can bring those tones together and create that beautifully reflective finish," he adds.

Alternatively, Reed recommends his “just a little filler” approach, using extensions as color tools placed strategically. This approach uses additional hair to enhance color, density, and movement, rather than focusing solely on length. "By carefully selecting complementary shades of chestnut, honey, and caramel, we can introduce tonal variation without necessarily having to chemically lighten the natural hair," he notes.

Whether you're looking for a complete hair refresh this winter or simply want a subtle way to enhance your natural color without fully committing to a new look, Halliwell's dark honey hair is the perfect inspiration.

#6827. The Princess of Wales Swaps Her Classic Waves for a Romantic Boho Hairstyle: How to Recreate It

During her three-day tour of India, the Princess of Wales attended the 2026 Earthshot Prize Ceremony in Mumbai. Prince William founded the initiative in 2020, and it awards environmental solutions with grants to scale and advance their projects.

For the event, Kate wore an oxblood, floor-length Anarkali dress by designer Mahima Mahajan and paired it with extra-fluffy, voluminous bohemian curls pinned in a half-updo.

An elegant, extra-glossy wavy hairstyle has long been a beauty signature for the princess, often teamed with a side part, hats, or a polished ponytail. But for the green carpet in India, the royal opted for a much more dramatic style that matches the glamorous mood of her outfit, with tighter, cascading bohemian curls woven into a beautiful half-updo twist for a romantic finish.

"I would create these curls using a medium-sized tong," recommends Philipp Haug, co-founder of Haug London Haus salon. "Take relatively small sections and use the tongs with a twisting technique, curling one section away from the face and alternating with one section towards the face throughout. Slightly root stretch when you come to the top of the head instead of curling too close to the scalp."

To enhance the fluffiness and volume of the curls, shake them out with your fingers instead of combing them.

Your next special occasion hair inspiration, sorted.

#6822. Jennifer Lawrence Sets the Record Straight on Those Blepharoplasty Rumors

The actress credits her makeup artist, Shayna Gold, as the mastermind behind her “bleph”

In this day and age, it’s almost a rite of passage for a celebrity to have plastic surgery rumors about them swirling online, but Jennifer Lawrence has been speaking her truth on the matter as of late.

Last week on Watch What Happens Live, the Hunger Games actress casually slipped into her conversation with host Andy Cohen that she has new breast implants and even credited her plastic surgeon, Steven Teitelbaum, for the “full C” cups.

She continues to set the record straight on her cosmetic procedures—this time shutting down rumors that she had a blepharoplasty. Online rumors quickly speculated that Lawrence had undergone upper eyelid surgery, but her latest Instagram post shuts them down. Her makeup artist, Shayna Gold, shared a mailer of products from the actress’s new haircare line, Mes, and Lawrence reposted the photo to her own story with a cheeky caption. “Iykyk she’s the one who did my bleph,” Lawrence wrote, crediting Gold and pointing to makeup as the reason she looks so wide awake, officially poking holes in any eyelid surgery rumors.

Lawrence has previously been open about her plastic surgery. In October, after gracing the cover of The Cut, she revealed she had work done following the birth of her second child, which the publication then specified as a boob job. One year earlier, the actress told The New Yorker, “Everything bounced back, pretty much, after the first one. Second one, nothing bounced back.”

While fans aren’t exactly sold on Lawrence’s denial of a bleph, makeup artists often do have tricks up their sleeves for faking an eyelid lift. For starters, placement matters. When emphasizing the crease, apply shadow above the natural crease. “I like patting shadows onto the lid first, then blending the edges after. Too much back-and-forth blending can make the shape disappear,” celebrity makeup artist Christian Briceno previously told Bazaar. Also, opt for an elongated look, like a soft wing, to make the eye look instantly bigger. This also applies to mascara—artists recommend focusing application on the outer corners versus the center.

Among her favorites from the holiday collections is the Fate Star Gold Short Pendant Necklace, a reversible, sculptural star designed to be worn on its own or layered with other pieces.

Below, Skye shares her holiday gifting philosophy, along with her curated selection of gifts for everyone on your list (including yourself).

“I love gifting pieces that feel timeless but still have a little personality. This cuff is something she can wear every day or dress up for a special occasion.”

#6815. Maple Bourbon Chicken With Brussels Sprouts

If you, like most people, hear the term “bourbon chicken” and have no idea what it is, let me call it by its more common name: toothpick chicken. Yes, the suspiciously delicious chicken shelled out at mall food courts on toothpicks is most likely bourbon chicken. Popular at American Chinese or Cajun-style Chinese restaurants, the sticky-sweet chicken is the utter definition of delicious, luring weary shoppers into food courts to find refuge from the bubblegum pink sweatpants and ripped denim. This version takes cues from the classic but swaps in some autumnal ingredients to make it fit for the season. Charred brussels sprouts leaves and pecans add bulk, while maple syrup and apple cider vinegar give it that delightful tart-sweet flavor we know and love. Serve it over rice or noodles or all by its lonesome for a hearty dinner that will take you back to the '90s.

More chicken dinner winners here: Sheet-Pan Hot Honey Chicken and Peaches; Basil Chicken Stir-Fry; and Ginger-Garlic Chicken and Vegetable Stir-Fry.

Whisk one 1½" piece ginger, peeled, finely grated, 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, ⅓ cup bourbon, ⅓ cup pure maple syrup, ¼ cup apple cider vinegar, ¼ tsp. crushed red pepper flakes, 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, ¼ cup low-sodium soy sauce, and ½ cup water in a small bowl to combine. Set sauce aside.

Toss 1½ lb. skinless, boneless chicken breasts, halved crosswise, thinly sliced against the grain, remaining 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, and remaining 1 Tbsp. low-sodium soy sauce in a medium bowl until chicken is coated; set aside.

Heat 2 Tbsp. vegetable oil in a large skillet over medium-high. Add 1 lb. brussels sprouts, halved through stems, leaves separated, and cook, stirring very occasionally, until just tender and blackened in spots, about 5 minutes. Transfer to another medium bowl.

Add more oil if pan looks dry and, working in batches if needed, arrange reserved chicken in a single layer and cook, turning occasionally, until golden brown and cooked through, about 5 minutes. Transfer to bowl with brussels sprouts.

Add reserved sauce to same pan and bring to a simmer. Cook, stirring constantly, until thickened, about 1 minute. Remove pan from heat and return brussels sprouts and chicken to pan; top with 1 cup salted roasted pecans, coarsely chopped. Toss to coat in sauce. Serve chicken and brussels sprouts with steamed white rice if desired.

#6813. All Aboard Fashion’s Absurd Hat Train

Swim caps and train conductor hats: The runway’s most theatrical headwear is made for standing out.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fashion can’t get enough of freaky hats lately. Following the main character hat trend from a few seasons past, this time headwear has gone totally theatrical and over-the-top. Between Chanel’s fin-embellished Nemo-esque swim caps and Junya Watanabe’s super camp, oversize ’70s baker-boy-style caps, headpieces are seemingly obsessed with transforming their wearers into outlandish personas. And at a time when clothing is centered on escapism and storytelling, an eccentric chapeau might just be the most perfect finishing touch.

Simone Rocha’s sequin-embellished bonnets with chin straps might turn you into a living doll. Celebs, too, have already gotten the memo: Elle Fanning recently wore a Stephen Jones-designed makeup compact headpiece to match her Givenchy dress inspired by an archival piece he created over three decades ago, while doing press for The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping. Elsewhere, Cardi B showed up in a white embellished gown from Tamara Ralph’s spring 2026 couture collection, styled with a huge, showgirl-style feather hat. Ciara walked the Junya Watanabe spring/summer 2027 runway while wearing a bedazzled helmet dripping with chains.

At Vaquera’s spring/summer 2027 show titled “I’m a put-on-a-show kind of girl,” the neon high-octane gowns were counterbalanced with huge, satirical boater hats, followed by a weird and whimsical chef hat and maid headpiece paired with shrunken, mini bloomer uniform onesies. Adding more humor to the collection, the designers ended the show by performing a live karaoke version of Sonny & Cher’s “I Got You Babe.” The brand is the king of the theatrical character hat. For fall/winter 2026, it showcased pillbox-style hats that eclipsed the face with cutouts for the eyes, fencing-mask-inspired headwear, and an elongated train conductor hat that resembles a cross between The Rockettes Toy Soldier costumes and English bearskin caps.

Transforming is exactly the point: “Vaquera has always celebrated fashion’s transformative power,” read Vaquera’s show notes. “The collection confronts the tension between perfection and chaos. Can you be unhinged and complete at once? Its cast of characters—distorted drab mods, twisted ladies who lunch, abstracted leather daddies—all seem to suggest that the most covetable clothes for your future are those that mirror our fractured present.”

While years of the past were defined by singular aesthetics, 2026 lacks one solo homogeneous style. Formality, too, has been traded for casual looks. Hats used to be ubiquitous; now they’re sort of a rare dying breed, save for a baseball cap.

Perhaps that’s one of the reasons why these character-driven hats are so prevalent now. Many of them are tied to uniforms, occupations, or the everyday made extreme. It’s beautiful absurdism, verging on delightfully unhinged. Consider an over-the-top hat pure whimsy.

#6811. The Art of the Winter Layer

Use these cold-weather staples from The North Face for practical warmth with polished style.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like any specialized skill set, winter layering is one that must be continually practiced and perfected, lest your talents wane. It’s not as easy as throwing on a pair of cashmere leggings and a coordinating hoodie—that’s only the beginning. From there, it takes a careful curation of hero pieces (like puffer jackets and parkas), as well as a cast of supporting details (hats, gloves, and high-performance footwear), to layer effectively and aesthetically in the harshest conditions.

That said, even the most adept artist is nothing without an arsenal of trusted creative tools. And in these bitter temps, you’ll want to reach for the best, which essentially means any piece of gear with The North Face’s iconic rectangular logo attached to it. The storied brand has dominated the outdoor space for decades, having perfected the art of elevated style at elevated altitudes.

As we head into the chilliest season of the year, look to The North Face’s vast selection of dependable staples for all your layering needs. Designed both for performance and appearances, these pieces are in it for the long run.

Whether painting or dressing, it’s essential to start any creative endeavor with a blank canvas made of quality materials. A good base should be versatile and simple to pair with a host of helpful staples. Long-sleeve tees, cashmere turtlenecks, and crewneck sweatshirts, for example, are all good jumping-off points.

If you’re looking for a high-performance piece with enough style power to stand on its own, we recommend the Women’s Chabot 2.0 Mock-Neck. This cozy layering top offers the same reliable comfort of a traditional insulated thermal with a few modern updates. Though understated by design, its delightfully oversized fit and a funky waffle knit texture make the basic feel intentional and interesting.

For a closer-fitting base, the Women’s Golden Ridge Ribbed Long-Sleeve Tee layers easily under other pieces. Its slim fit and ribbed texture give this practical layer a polished look, while thumbholes at the cuffs add a functional detail. Bonus points for the tee’s anti-odor technology that helps keep the fabric feeling fresh.

Women’s Golden Ridge Ribbed Long-Sleeve Tee

After you’ve selected the first layer, the next stop should be your coat closet. This is where you’ll select the hero of your look. While each piece of the layering puzzle is uniquely important, the power jacket is a non-negotiable. The right iteration should supply a thick layer of warmth, while also doing its part to make the outfit, as a whole, feel cohesive.

Those who crave a more subdued winter aesthetic will love the classic appeal of the Women’s Arctic Bomber. The waterproof winter jacket boasts all the utility of a parka, with the sleek design details most commonly found in trend-focused outerwear. In four comforting colorways, this winter coat is an insulation powerhouse in a chic, wearable package.

Look to the Women’s Hydrenalite Down Jacket for a more classic puffer. This workhorse is a versatile down layer that can be slipped on at any time to keep you warm for any duration that you spend outdoors, whether you are on the mountain or running around town.

For the more artistically inclined, try the Women’s Yumiori Off Peak Full Zip Jacket. This fleece outer layer offers unfailing warmth and a distinctly sporty flavor. Its two-tone color palette, meanwhile, adds a fun, whimsical feel to the utilitarian, zip-up-style silhouette.

During the summer months, it’s all about the mani-pedi. But once that icy weather blows in, self-care starts to look a bit different. The North Face’s Etip Gloves are basically a hand treatment on the go. Crafted from soft, stretchy fleece made with 93 percent recycled polyester, these tech-friendly gloves will keep your hands comfortable on cold-weather outings. They’ll also keep you connected: Touchscreen compatibility lets you check your phone without taking them off, while silicone grip dots on the palms help you hold onto your device as well as smaller essentials like keys and credit cards.

For footwear, the Women’s THERMOBALL Traction Mule V will quickly become the standout of your seasonal lineup. These cloud-like slip-ons pair warm insulation with a quilted upper that gives them a casual, streetwear-cool vibe. The rubber outsole adds traction for quick trips outdoors.

The minimalist philosophy requires you to take off one item before leaving the house. But during the cold weather season, it’s in your best interest to do the opposite. As you walk out the door, make sure you grab a beanie since most of your body heat can escape from your head.

Beanies are simultaneously the most underappreciated outfit addition and the most indispensable. The North Face’s Wool Blend Beanie, for instance, pairs the warmth of its 65 percent merino wool knit with a refined, metropolitan style. Its classic cuffed design and textured knit add visual interest, while its deep fit offers roomier coverage. If you prefer the rectangular logo with the same cuffed look, you can never go wrong with the rib-knit and soft lining of the Salty Lined Beanie. On the slopes or on your morning commute, either will add a polished finishing touch.

The importance of a functional winter hat cannot be overstated—think of it as the final brushstroke in the art of winter layering.

#6806. The Coolest Fashion Hub of the ’00s Was in a SoHo Basement

A new book captures the It girl scene at 163 Mercer Street

Back when Marc Jacobs was dressing the coolest girls of the aughts, retail wunderkind Robert Rich was his secret weapon. Not unlike Truman Capote and his "swans," Rich welcomed friends such as Kate Moss, Selma Blair, Sofia Coppola, Maya Rudolph, Natasha Lyonne, Parker Posey, and Winona Ryder to his basement office in the brand’s downtown flagship store, documenting fittings and gab sessions with a Polaroid camera. Now, 163 Mercer, a new book from IDEA named for the store’s address, shares stories and photographs of fashion’s golden years just before digital changed everything.

Rich's first book, Winona, made with Francesca Sorrenti, debuted in 2023 after he spotted merchandise on IDEA’s Instagram feed emblazoned with the name “Winona.” "I reached out and asked if they wanted to do a Winona Polaroid book, and they immediately replied, 'Yes!'" he recalled over Zoom. It sold out on day one, and a second printing followed the next year. The experience left him wanting more creative outlets.

163 Mercer is “directed” by Zoe Cassavetes, the director, screenwriter, and actress, who was the sales director of the nearby Mercer Hotel in the ’90s. She met Rich when Jacobs and Marc Jacobs International president Robert Duffy sent her to the store to pick out a thank-you gift for her professional hospitality at the Mercer Hotel, where Jacobs often stayed.

The duo, who are so close that they still speak on the phone every day, began brainstorming a project. "I had the photos of all the other girls, so I knew I wanted to do a book with those pictures," Rich said. They considered wallpaper based on the wall collage or a podcast featuring a different one of the girls per episode, but Cassavetes's film background, including her 2007 directorial debut Broken English, made her the perfect book creative partner. IDEA said yes again.

They worked at Cassavetes’s Los Angeles apartment, laying out images on her oversized office table. "It's a lot like writing a script, which is why I say I ‘directed’ the book; putting together the layout was like making a movie," she said. Rich occasionally asked her to add an edited image, and she agreed. "We have similar tastes, and we were open to each other's opinions. He has a cool eye," she added.

Their bond developed when Cassavetes worked down the street. "My title at the Mercer was director of sales, but I was really a VIP manager. I had a basement office, too," she recalled. Before New York's indoor smoking ban in 2003, the two took cigarette breaks indoors. "The logistics helped our friendship, hanging out in each other's offices smoking and having tea."

The book features images of regular visitors plus some who came once or twice, and the women graciously cataloged their memories for it. Don't expect captions throughout; it reads in parts like an IYKYK story asking viewers to figure out who is in the photo. "We didn't caption every Polaroid; they weren't like social media posts," Rich explained.

Nostalgia for an era before our feeds made the post-Covid years the perfect time for Rich to revisit his mementos. The book should appeal to members of Gen Z who are wondering what they missed before social media, and those in Gen X who are longing for a time that felt authentic, rather than conceived in a marketing strategy.

Trust bound everyone involved. Rich knew Moss previously, and Cassavetes knew Sofia and Ryder; Rich met the others on the job. Ryder came into the shop independently, and Rich professed his love of Girl, Interrupted. “’That's a girl's movie,’ she said, and I said, ‘I'm a girl's movie guy,’” Rich recalled. Ryder's infamous 2001 shoplifting case led to her starring in the brand's spring 2003 campaign, appearing on its "Protect the Skin You're In" T-shirts and wearing Marc Jacobs to court.

"Selma didn't know the others,” Rich said. “We hit it off when she tried on the Courrèges–inspired collection. I took Polaroids, sent some to Marc, and kept others. She kept coming in. I introduced her to Kate, Missy, and Frankie Rayder, all of them.”

The mood differed from today's brand-celebrity ecosystem of mutual monetary gain. "It was unpretentious; no one was there to get paid. We were hanging out and having fun. That was the Marc Jacobs spirit: more artsy, less corporate. It was a perfect zeitgeist moment," Cassavetes said.

Case in point: Rich met Hilary Swank before her Oscar win for Boys Don't Cry. "She would come to the store, and I would dress her up for all the pre-Oscar award shows and events. I put her in a tiered ruffle sundress with a beachy vibe for the Independent Spirit Award in 2000," he recalled.

Impromptu gatherings were plentiful. Once, Moss, Ryder, and Patric Reeves ended up in the office together. "I stocked my fridge with Sofia champagne cans. They were small but potent," Rich said with a chuckle. An image in the book documents the mini party. Though Polaroids waned in popularity after the brand declared bankruptcy and digital cameras and camera phones arrived, Rich kept a stockpile he used until 2015.

"Sometimes I wouldn't even recognize Winona because she had a new role, hair color, or style," he said. "But I met all the boyfriends, watched them get married, pregnant, have babies, and divorce."

Getting It girls to wear Marc Jacobs was simple. "Marc's clothes were unbelievable; you didn't have to beg them," said Rich, adding that the store's cool yet friendly vibe helped. "I was a big fan of Marc's, and that radiated to everyone I hired," including bartenders, who made great sales associates because "they knew how to talk to people." A generous clothing allowance, four outfits a season instead of uniforms, fueled employee enthusiasm.

Cassavetes remembers Rich's relaxed styling approach. "He made sure the girls didn't wear the same thing." Rich credits his former boss. "Marc liked everybody's take on his clothes. Winona would change the buttons or redo straps; he loved it."

Costume designer June Ambrose's favorite memories include discovering hidden treasures in the basement. "Robert has an incredible eye and instinctively knew what I needed; I could trust him. He understood the woman, the attitude, and the personality behind the clothes," she wrote in an email. "He made me fall in love with Marc Jacobs."

Rich also gave his editor and stylist friends early access to sample sales.

His Marc Jacobs career began when Joe Zee took him to his first runway show for the brand. "I was blown away and marched backstage and asked Marc for a job," Rich said. In 1999, he became store manager, eventually rising to vice president of public relations for retail stores during his tenure.

Before 163 Mercer became the Marc Jacobs store in 1997, it was once the Tony Shafrazi Gallery, which propelled the careers of Francis Bacon, David LaChapelle, and Keith Haring, for whom Rich once worked. "There was nothing in SoHo; Marc could have picked any space. There was good energy there. Tony would stop in and regret having given the space up," Rich said, noting Jacobs kicked off SoHo’s current designer shopping enclave.

"The store was so laid-back and a fun, creative safe space. It was also the center of the downtown fashion world at that time," Cassavetes added.

Rich hints that a third book would move away from SoHo. "We wouldn't just be in the office. Maybe it would be pictures from everywhere."

#6801. The 70+ Best Black Friday Deals We’re Shopping This Year

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Sales are all over the place, but we found the ones worth acting on.

Thanksgiving weekend is for spending quality time with family—catching up, passing the potatoes, and fielding at least one question about your job or love life. It’s also a pretty great opportunity to shop Black Friday deals, which tend to be the best sales of the year. But when you’re surrounded by people you only see once or twice a year, you probably don’t want to spend hours scouring the Internet for the best price on a new pair of headphones.

That’s where we come in. We’ve rounded up more than 70 of the best Black Friday deals from our favorite brands, with substantial discounts on tech, menswear, fragrance, bedding, furniture, travel essentials, and so much more. Consider this your shortcut to all the good stuff—and a way to spend more time with your loved ones. And don’t forget to ask them what they want for the holidays so you can knock out all your holiday gift shopping while everything is marked down.

Black Friday used to be a big in-person event where people would get into physical altercations over the last shirt on the rack at the mall, but now it’s more of a month-long thing that happens mostly online. And when it comes to online shopping, there’s one mega-retailer we all know (and love to hate, or hate to love): Amazon. It’s no surprise that Amazon is one of the main places we’re looking for deals, and there are some pretty sweet markdowns on Apple tech, watches, Lego sets, and Kindles.

#6797. How Does Owen Wilson Find His Zen?

“I just believe there’s something more going on that you can tap into,” the actor tells Esquire on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.”

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Owen Wilson is a spiritual guy—and long has been. That’s why it fits so naturally that he would star in a golf series for Apple TV, Stick, in which his character, Pryce Cahill, is often searching to find his perfect zen. And as Wilson jokes, he’s started calling himself “Johnny Flowstate” on the green as he talks to himself through shots.

“I just believe there’s something more going on that you can tap into,” he tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The fifty-five-year-old actor, best known for his roles in The Royal Tenenbaums, Wedding Crashers, and Midnight in Paris, even admits that he found himself reaching into the beyond for advice from his late father, TV executive Robert Wilson, in a moment of pure panic before hosting Saturday Night Live in 2021.

“He was already gone at that point, but the night before I was freaking out like, I don’t know if I can do this. I was so nervous, and I was thinking a lot about my dad,” Wilson says. The two were incredibly close, and he reveals that his father actually worked with him and his brothers, actors Luke Wilson and Andrew Wilson, on several of their films. One of which was Wes Anderson’s Rushmore, in which their father wrote Bill Murray’s now-iconic church address to the students: “Take dead aim on the rich boys."

So, as Wilson found himself full of doubt and fear the night before hosting SNL, he recalls looking up and “literally asking [his father] for help” before falling asleep. “I woke up, just incredibly calm, and I had this conviction of this joke my dad would say that needed to go in my monologue.”

What was the joke? “That he was the charter member of the 3970 club,” Wilson says, “which stood for ‘three sons, nine high schools, seven colleges, and zero degrees.’”

“That wasn’t something he was proud of,” Wilson continues. “That was his way to cope with disappointment, but you could almost say that that joke was worth us having all those struggles in school because that joke has lived longer than another diploma would have.” You can watch the episode above as well as listen on Spotify and Apple Podcasts.

Luckily, it all worked out for the Wilson brothers. Next up, Owen is set to appear in season 2 of Stick on Apple TV and the Meet the Parents legacy sequel, Focker-in-Law with Ben Stiller. He also takes a liking to Esquire’s suggestion of a Christmas movie with another one of his longtime collaborators, Wes Anderson, in the episode.

“It’s one of the biggest relationships of my life,” Wilson says of his friendship with the director. “Had I gotten into one of the colleges that I applied to, I would never have met Wes. And there’s something I always say to my boys—the idea that you can’t do it alone. If there’s something going wrong, I know I can always call and he’ll always be there for me.”

#6796. The 10 Best Lipsticks for Women Over 40 That Deliver Instant Hydration and Lasting Color

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

A swipe of lipstick is one of the easiest ways to look instantly put together with minimal effort. Even if you have nothing else on your face, a hint of lip color can lift your complexion. It also works as an accessory or finishing touch for even the simplest outfits.

As we get older, lips naturally feel drier and lose volume. A good balm nourishes and comforts parched lips, but if you want more pigment, many lipsticks are designed for mature skin.

First, steer clear of ultra-matte formulas, as they tend to dehydrate lips and highlight every fine lip line imaginable. They also tend to look flat.

For extra long wear, try a velvety, soft-matte formula instead. It delivers a similar, statement-making finish that's elegant while creating a pillowy, plush effect.

Satin formulas perform well, too. The gentle shine creates the illusion of healthier-looking lips, and it feels less drying throughout the day. That said, brighter shades like reds and oranges can be a little messy to apply, so apply one layer, blend with a fingertip, and blot with a tissue. Repeat to build intensity. If feathering (when the color "bleeds" into fine lines around your lips) is an issue, a clear lip liner around the perimeter should create a barrier.

It's always best to get your skincare benefits from your lip balm, but nourishing plant oils and hydrating ingredients such as glycerin and hyaluronic acid in your lipstick will help maintain hydration as the day goes on.

For makeup artist and Jones Road founder Bobbi Brown, there are no hard-and-fast rules—no matter your age. "It's about your style, what you feel most comfortable in," she tells Bazaar UK.

If you're keen on neutral tones, remember that neutral doesn't mean beige. "Pay attention to what your natural lip color is. I have a slightly bluish-purple tone to my lips. You might have more of a pink tone. It's really great to find a lipstick that really looks like your lips, and the only way is to test it on your bottom lip," says Brown, who notes that a great neutral should not wash you out.

Revered makeup artist Bobbi Brown is no stranger to creating iconic lipsticks. The Classic Lip has all the hallmarks of a modern formula: creamy, intensely hydrating, and richly pigmented. The 12-color range offers enough variety without overwhelming consumers. Expect skin-enhancing neutrals and bolder tones for any occasion.

Trinny London excels at makeup for mature skin, and this recent launch is certainly no exception. It's a glossy, mega-hydrating formula that reflects light beautifully, making lips look plumper, and it's formulated with peptides to help reduce fine lines over time. There's a full spectrum of nude shades, but we love the bold red Poppy for festive nights.

A richly pigmented, buttery-smooth formula that promises to minimize dryness without sacrificing impact. It contains plenty of nourishing oils to keep moisture lasting without feathering into fine lines. It has a rosy scent, so keep that in mind if you're sensitive to fragrance.

Everything Victoria Beckham does delivers instant polish to the lips without extra effort. This slimline lipstick is no exception. It glides on like a tinted lip balm, adding a touch more color while infusing skin with hydration. VB has perfected the art of neutrals, so expect to find easy-to-wear hues for all skin tones. Glide on, then pat to blend any hard edges. It's one of those formulas you won't need a mirror to apply.

Refillable makeup isn't new, but Lisa Eldridge has painstakingly ensured every part of her Rouge Experience lipstick is recyclable, while it still looks like it belongs on a movie star's dresser. The lipstick formula combines oil, gel, and cream to ensure all-day comfort. The color pigments build easily and sit between a soft matte and a shiny finish. It's unfragranced, too.

Creative and image director of Christian Dior Makeup, Peter Philips, reviewed Dior's bestselling lip colors worldwide to create a tightly curated collection of 10 sophisticated, wearable shades for every skin tone. Nude doesn't mean beige, according to Philips, and this line includes a complexion-lifting neutral, whatever that looks like to you. The emollient formula glides on like balmy, satin lipstick but won't budge once it sets. Expect long-wear color without the drying side effects.

When Hourglass creates matte lipsticks, rest assured they feature elevated colors and sit beautifully on the lips without highlighting fine lines, feathering, or flaking. This elegant formula feels velvety soft, with buildable pigments that stay put.

Sisley's interpretation of a shimmery, luminous lip color is as elevated as you've come to expect from this storied French brand. Phyto Rouge Shine is infused with the finest pearlescent particles in a translucent base, delivering sheer, buildable color and a plumping effect that still looks understated and grown-up.

Hermès Beauty creative director Gregoris Pyrpylis drew inspiration from Hermès scarves when designing this collection. This translates into buildable, luminous color and a smooth, tactile finish that feels like a second skin. Even the deepest shades look refined, with a radiance that brightens the face. Plus, the wide, angled bullet makes application as easy as wearing a lip balm.

Not all liquid lipsticks are created equal. Violette's soft, whipped texture is truly modern, delivering intense colors without a heavy feel. The gel-cream texture blurs out fine lines, too, so lips appear plush and smooth.

#6794. Why The Rings of Power Will Never Be the Same After the Season 3 Finale

A post-finale interview with the showrunners of the ‘Lord of the Rings’ series reveals what may come.

When the creators and showrunners of The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power embarked on season 3, they knew the endgame from the start: The forging of the One Ring. After two seasons in the Second Age of Middle-earth, which saw almost all the other Rings of Power forged—three for Elves, seven for Dwarves, and nine for Men—it was time for Sauron to forge the one to rule them all.

“We wanted a tighter, tauter, more profound season,” says co-showrunner Patrick McKay, in an interview with Esquire. “We wanted to start with a bang and never let up. We wanted to deepen our characters psychologically, and challenge them in new ways. You’re seeing the end of one part of the story, and the beginning of another.”

“We had to have Sauron forge the One Ring,” adds J.D. Payne, who also created and serves as co-showrunner with McKay. “To do that, all the storylines in some way are connected to Sauron’s attempts at world domination. That was the one that so much of our attention and focus went into getting right, and it took all of our departments working together at the top of their games to create.”

On Wednesday, just in time for Thanksgiving, Prime Video gave Tolkien fans a feast with the final two episodes of The Rings of Power season 3: episode 7, “Orodruin,” and episode 8, “The Doom of Men.”

In the penultimate episode, Sauron (Charlie Vickers) learns from the Balrog—who talks, apparently!—how to use the fires of Mount Doom to forge the One Ring. Then, with the power of the One Ring literally in his hands, Sauron mobilizes his army for battle against Pharazôn (Trystan Gravelle), the king of Númenor. But instead of a big, violent bloodbath in the spirit of, say, Game of Thrones, Sauron simply... surrenders. Or does he? We’re talking about a master deceiver, after all.

In a spoiler-filled interview with Esquire, McKay and Payne unpack a few of our biggest questions about season 3, including how and why the Balrog speaks (with actor Simon Pegg lending his voice) to why Pharazôn is actually one of the most consequential figures in the series.

This interview has been edited for clarity.

PATRICK MCKAY: One of the things we're constantly trying to do is go deeper into Tolkien’s life's work. He did not just create The Lord of the Rings, he wrote all this other stuff. He envisioned an entire world. It's his legendarium. When you do the deep dive, you find there are surprising things if you only know the book and film trilogy. The Balrog Gandalf faces down in Moria is part of this level of “super being” that is equal to Gandalf and Sauron. They’re Maiar. In prior ages, Balrogs could cast spells and command troops. They’re intelligent. They can talk, you just don’t see that one talk in that circumstance.

The idea of walking out onto a diving board and doing something risky, to show a different side of that being, was something that we felt compelled to do this season. It’s certainly unexpected. We’re always looking to do that. We don’t want to be familiar. We want to show you things in this world that you haven’t seen before.

Talk to me about designing a new One Ring. Granted, it’s a gold ring; there’s not much you can really deviate. But in what ways did you want to create a One Ring that belongs to The Rings of Power?

MCKAY: The books are pretty specific about the way it looks. It’s a plain gold band. If you’re gonna do that, it’s going to look like the One Ring you’ve seen in other adaptations. We did make a few modifications, just for camera. We broadened it to give it more chunkiness. But it is what it says on the tin.

How is season 3 a culmination of everything that’s happened before, and how is it setting up the rest of the show?

J.D. PAYNE: It pulls together everything we’ve been doing with Sauron and Galadriel from the first two seasons. In season 1, he befriended her and deceived her in the guise of Halbrand. In season 3, you see Celebrimbor tormenting him: “You’re never gonna be able to make a ring capable of dominating all the other rings—or dominating her.” Part of Sauron’s ambition to forge the One Ring, as a character motivation, is being able to dominate the person who said “no” to him over the last few seasons. You understand how crazy that must have driven him.

MCKAY: The show’s called Rings of Power, because the creation is the throughline of this entire era of Tolkien’s mythology. At the end of season 3, you’ve now seen the creation of all of them. That’s now the world we live in. Everyone in Middle-earth is a nuclear power, so to speak. Now we’re setting up for the next part of the story, which is, “What are the consequences going to be?” One thing in season 3 we really invested in was Númenor to set Sauron up as the bowling ball crashing into those pins. That’s sort of the beginning of the end of the Second Age. It’s a tragic story. The Rings have an impact on Middle-earth that is felt for thousands of years. Now they are in this world.

The finale is literally titled “The Doom of Men,” and it ends with Sauron “surrendering” to Pharazôn. Or so that’s what he wants them to think. Can you break down this moment? How is this Sauron’s big chess move?

PAYNE: Everything builds up to that. You’ve seen Gandalf getting through to the Dark Wizard and take out a massive portion of Sauron’s army at the last moment. So the game changes right from underneath him, and he has to make a calculation. Is he going to put all his chips on the table and lose his army in a stalemate? Or is there another way? The great thing about Sauron is that he’s amazing at constructing scenarios that are lose-lose. If he can’t beat Pharazôn on the field of battle, he reasons maybe there’s another way. That’s why the final moment is filled with tension. It’s not simply a surrender. Our Elves, particularly Galadriel, know Sauron so well. She knows this is not a defeat for Sauron, and he’s going to find a way to make this into a play for exactly what he wants. Those who know the lore know what happens when Sauron is taken prisoner to Númenor. We wanted that feeling of dread, watching Charlie Vickers have a hint of a smile.

One of the most consequential characters this season has been Pharazôn. In what ways does Pharazôn represent the downfall of man?

MCKAY: This is a major character in the mythology. We introduced him in season 1, he’s been sort of a politician gathering power. In season 3, he’s been king for five days, and he’s in his heyday. He’s in his golden era. But he’s driven by insecurity. There was this blacksmith we empowered, who got away, who’s now marching around saying he’s “Lord.” No, I’m the guy! There’s professional jealousy there.

We talk sometimes about how he’s Stalin to Sauron’s Hitler. You’ve got two characters who are quite full of themselves, and have a high opinion of themselves, which puts them on a trajectory. Pharazôn’s goal is, “I want to take on Sauron.” That makes him a compelling character because you watch the way he greases the wheels and maneuvers to bring about that end. We give a lot of credit to our actor, Trystan Gravelle. He leaps off the screen in season 3.

Speaking of showdowns, one of the biggest confrontations you stage in season 3 is between Sauron and Gandalf, which Tolkien never wrote in the books. What did you hope to discover by pitting these characters together?

MCKAY: The journey of Gandalf and his involvement with Ciaran Hinds’ character, Pollando, has real deep roots in some Tolkien deep cuts where he talked about wizards who went east and that their activities probably prevented the east from joining Sauron. The story comes from that. We wanted to dramatize that through some of the most iconic characters in the legendarium coming face to face.

PAYNE: Seeing the contrast between them felt like too delicious an opportunity to pass up. Sauron is seeking power at all costs, and Gandalf has been told by the Secret Fire, “To access the greatest power in the universe, you have to give up all other power.” So when Gandalf is offered the world by Sauron, he turns it down. If you demand power, it’s going to destroy you. Ultimately, having the One Ring, and forging it and trying to keep power, sets up the weaknesses of the Third Age that the heroes exploit to finally destroy Sauron.

One of my favorite hobbies is Magic: The Gathering, which has had both Lord of the Rings and The Hobbit as crossover sets. Are there any possibilities for a Rings of Power collaboration?

MCKAY: [Laughs.] No. This is the first we’ve heard about it.

PAYNE: We actually wrote a draft of a Magic: The Gathering script in the before times. It was one of the last scripts we worked on before turning to Rings of Power. But it was right before the Disney-Fox merger, and it kinda got lost there. But we love that universe.

#6787. The Secrets Behind Brittany Snow’s Press-Tour Glow

The actress is embracing a more intentional beauty routine with a little help from RéVive Skincare.

Though her recent red-carpet looks may beg to differ, Brittany Snow is no longer chasing perfection—at least when it comes to her appearance. Instead, the actress, who turned 40 this year and is starring in one of the moment’s most deliciously bingeable series, now in its second season, is focused on aging well and nourishing her skin with intention. “There’s something really freeing about realizing you don’t need to look perfect to feel confident,” Snow says. “I spent so much of my younger years unfortunately being aware of how I looked. Now, I’m much more interested in just looking like myself, just hopefully a little more hydrated.” That’s where RéVive comes in.

Snow credits her makeup artist Charlie Riddle with introducing her to the luxe skin care brand and helping her evolve her relationship to beauty. “He has always focused on making my skin look like skin—healthy, hydrated, and luminous,” she says. “He understands that the better you take care of your skin, the less you need to cover it up.”

Throughout the current press tour, Riddle has been prepping Snow’s skin with RéVive; the brand has quickly become a staple in her personal routine, too. “I was initially drawn to how beautiful my skin felt when Charlie used the products on me,” she says. “There was this sense of hydration without feeling like I had a million things sitting on my face. Charlie has taught me that the edit matters; I used to be someone who thought more products meant better results, and now I realize that consistency and quality are much more important.”

In addition to the products’ elegant feel and innovative, science-based formulas, Snow also appreciates RéVive’s brand story. “RéVive was founded by a plastic surgeon whose research began with helping burn victims heal,” she says. “There’s something really meaningful to me about a beauty brand that was born out of wanting to help people.”

Whether she’s preparing to film a scene, gearing up for a marathon press day, or decompressing before bed at home, she relies on RéVive’s Renewal Moisturizing Night Cream and Renewal Rescue Elixir Oil to hydrate, care for, and rejuvenate her skin.

Both products are powered by exclusive peptides enhanced by RéVive RVGF Technology—a breakthrough delivery system that targets skin concerns with greater precision to maximize efficacy. The complementary formulas work to renew skin overnight to reveal a healthier-looking, more radiant, and glowing complexion by morning.

“My skin goes through so much between filming, travel, and wearing makeup for hours,” she says. “By the end of the day, I really want something that feels rich and helps my skin look refreshed. After using them, my skin feels like glass.”

Glowing from the inside out, her recent appearances—be they in character on our TV screens or in late-night talk show clips on our phones—are proof both of RéVive’s results and the confidence that comes from feeling good in one’s own skin.

#6786. Olivia Colman Just Debuted Winter's Most Elegant Hair Transformation With a New Jet-Black Color

While brunettes might need less frequent touch-ups than blondes, any chemically colored hair still needs care and attention. “The best way to treat freshly coloured hair at home is to always use a professional color protect shampoo and conditioner,” Green explained. “It’s also important to avoid using too much heat,” she added.

But first things first: Always get a consultation. Green shared: “I’d always advise having a consultation with a colorist before any color change, so they can work out what the best shade is for your skin tone.” Noted!

#6785. Forget Plymouth Rock This Thanksgiving. American History Is Better Reflected by a Different Stone.

Instead of staring at a boulder in a hole in the ground, drive 42 minutes west to the town of Berkley, where our country’s complicated past is plain to see.

Let me send out a little Thanksgiving advice to potential tourists planning to visit us in the Commonwealth (God save it!) in the future. If some local tells you to go to see Plymouth Rock, tell them you have tickets for a four-day Duck Tour of the Freedom Trail. I promise you: Seeing Paul Revere’s house four times is infinitely more interesting than seeing Plymouth Rock once.

First of all, Plymouth Rock is a boulder in a hole in the ground. Oh, there’s a classical templish thing above it, but it’s still a boulder in a hole in the ground. Second, there’s almost no historical evidence that any pilgrim feet ever trod upon the rock in question. None of the contemporaneous accounts mention it. It was a nonagenarian named Thomas Faunce who, in 1741, 121 years after the Mayflower arrived, decided that this was the rock on which all the Bradfords and their shipmates disembarked. It moved around Plymouth a lot in the early years. In 1774, it cracked in two, which some folks took as an ill omen for the burgeoning Revolution. (Pieces of it are in the Smithsonian) The year 1620 was carved into the boulder’s surface in 1880. In 1920, it found its permanent home. In 1990, writing in The New Yorker, John McPhee wrote of the rock:

Even Alexis de Tocqueville, America’s greatest tourist and, therefore, its primordial sucker, fell for the Rock’s charisma.

Damn, Alexis. Have some Madeira and chillez out, mon ami.

If you want to see a genuinely interesting boulder, drive 42 minutes west to the town of Berkley, where you will find Dighton Rock State Park. In another templish building, there is Dighton Rock. It is a six-sided, five-foot-high slab of sandstone. It is also adorned with ancient petroglyphic carvings of uncertain origin. There are depictions of animals and an assortment of lines and whorls and mysterious designs. In 1680, John Danforth got a look at the Dighton Rock and not even his recently bestowed Harvard degree could explain them. He made drawings of the carvings he could see. (At the time Danforth saw it, the rock was partially submerged in the Taunton River.) It also drew the interest of local celebrities like Cotton Mather and poet James Russell Lowell.

Danforth’s opinion was that the carvings were made by the local Native tribes, and he thought it depicted an ancient battle of some sort. (If there’s anything like a consensus among people who have studied the carvings, it’s Danforth’s.) However logical that might seem, it didn’t tamp down speculation as the years went by. Down through the centuries, the carvings have been attributed to Phoenicians, Carthaginians, Vikings, Portuguese adventurers, Chinese explorers, and Japanese mariners. And, of course, inevitably … aliens.

However, it was a professor of psychology from Brown University who had the right of it, I think. Edmund Delabarre was a well-respected psychological researcher in the first half of the 20th century. He also grew fascinated with the study of rock carvings in and around Narragansett Bay. Ultimately, his researches brought him to the shores of the Taunton River and to the Dighton Rock.

Ultimately, Delabarre dismissed all the other claims of origin and concluded that the carvings on the Dighton Rock were probably made by a crew sailing under the command of one Miguel Corte-Real, a Portuguese explorer who charted about 600 miles of coastline in and around the Canadian Maritimes. In 1502, Corte-Real set sail for the western Atlantic, seeking to discover what had become of his brother, Gaspar, who had not returned from a voyage to Newfoundland. Miguel vanished, too.

Delabarre’s contention was that Miguel had made it to New England, and he argued that the inscription was a form of abbreviated Latin that proclaimed Corte-Real to be “king of the Indians.” In his 2017 book The Place of Stone, Douglas Hunter pretty much demolished all of the outre interpretations of the carvings, arguing that the carvings were of Native origin, and that the other interpretations were elements in the erasure of Native history and the legacy of bloody colonization, of which, ironically, the settlement at Plymouth was Ground Zero. The people who didn’t step onshore on a rock in Plymouth launched one of the first campaigns of the greater American genocide. As Hunter wrote:

So there’s American history on the Thanksgiving of 2026, ground up between two stones, one a legend with a counterfeit past, and another a mystery with a stolen past. The latter is by far the more compelling. Its history encompasses all of the history, good and bad, of this appropriated continent. Edmund Delabarre was wrong about Miguel Corte-Real and the Portuguese, but he might have been more right than he knew when he wrote that Dighton Rock was a kind of test. In the ambiguity of its carvings at the time of Delabarre’s work, a person saw in them what he wanted to see. Not all of it was pretty, or ennobling, or triumphal. But it's all ours.

#6783. Steak Bites With Spinach Couscous

A traditional steak dinner can feel like too much for a weeknight: too expensive, too likely to trip the smoke alarm, too fussy to cook to a perfect medium-rare. But cut a steak into bite-size pieces and suddenly it transforms into a quick-cooking protein that’s no more demanding than chicken breast or tofu. Rub the steak with the spices and make the quick no-recipe yogurt sauce a few hours ahead if it’s helpful with time management. To account for the extra liquid thrown off by the spinach as it cooks, the water-to-couscous ratio in this recipe differs from that often found on the back of the bag (or box) so measure accordingly.

More quick dinners this way: Spicy Chicken Lettuce Wraps; Gochujang Chicken Stir-Fry; and Easy Fish Tacos With Spicy Sauce.

Place 1–1¼ lb. boneless New York strip steak (1–1½" thick), cut into 1" pieces, 1 Tbsp. dried oregano, 2 tsp. onion powder, 2 tsp. sugar, and 2 tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a medium bowl; season generously with freshly ground pepper and toss to coat. Let sit at least 10 minutes or cover and chill up to 8 hours (no need to bring to room temperature before cooking if chilling).

Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large high-sided skillet or medium Dutch oven with a tight-fitting lid over medium. Add 1 bunch scallions, thinly sliced, ½ cup finely chopped dill, 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, and a large pinch of kosher salt; cook, stirring occasionally, until scallions are softened, about 5 minutes. Add 10 oz. baby spinach in 2–3 handfuls, stirring and letting wilt slightly after each addition before adding more, 5–7 minutes total. Pour in 1 cup water and bring to a boil; season with more salt if needed. Add 1 cup Moroccan couscous and stir to break up any lumps. Remove from heat; quickly cover with lid and let sit 10 minutes.

Meanwhile, heat 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large skillet, preferably cast iron, over high. Arrange steak in a single layer; cook, undisturbed, until deeply browned underneath, 6–8 minutes. Reduce heat to medium; continue to cook, stirring often, until meat is just cooked through, about 1 minute more. Remove from heat. Add remaining 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, and a large pinch of kosher salt; toss to combine. (Residual heat from pan will cook out any raw garlic flavor.)

Uncover couscous and fluff with a fork to separate grains. Spoon into a large shallow bowl or onto a platter. Top with steak. Cut 1 lemon into quarters and squeeze juice from 1 quarter over steak. Scatter thinly sliced cucumber and some dill sprigs on top; drizzle with oil.

Combine 1 cup plain whole-milk yogurt and a generous pinch of kosher salt in a small bowl; squeeze juice from 2 lemon quarters into yogurt. Stir to combine. If sauce is thick, stir in water, a spoonful at a time, to loosen.

Serve steak and couscous with yogurt sauce and remaining lemon quarter and more lemon wedges for squeezing over.

#6774. Mussels With Garlicky Miso Broth

Mussels deserve your attention on weeknights. An affordable and accessible protein, they also happen to cook in less time than it takes to boil water. As the bivalves steam, they release a briny liquid, further perfuming the buttery, sake-fortified miso broth. Load up each bowl with a few good ladles, slurping every drop with an assist from crusty bread or warm rice. And why not enjoy the rest of the bottle of sake alongside your meal?

The seafood counter at your supermarket should sell rinsed and debarred mussels, but it’s easy to do at home. Discard any with cracked shells. Gently tap open raw mussels on a counter to see if they slowly close; if they do, those are okay to use. Swish them in a water-filled salad spinner or colander fitted inside a bowl, draining and refilling until water runs clear (give any especially dirty ones a scrub with a sponge). Use a towel to pull off the fibrous beard and set on a towel-lined tray in the fridge until you’re ready to cook.

More seafood dinners this way: Avgolemono Seafood Stew; Spaghetti alle Vongole; and Gorgeous Green Shrimp.

Place 2 Tbsp. scallions from 8 scallions, thinly sliced on a diagonal, in a small bowl and set aside for serving.

Melt 6 Tbsp. unsalted butter in a large pot over medium heat. Add 8 garlic cloves, thinly sliced, and remaining scallions to pot and cook, stirring often, until softened, about 4 minutes. Add 2 Tbsp. white miso paste and ½ tsp. crushed red pepper flakes (if using) and cook until miso is broken up and incorporated into butter, about 20 seconds. Pour in 1 cup dry sake or dry white wine and stir to combine, then add 3–3½ lb. mussels, scrubbed, debearded. Cover pot and increase heat to medium-high. Cook, shaking pot occasionally, until mussels open, 5–7 minutes (discard any that do not open). Remove pot from heat.

Squeeze 1 lemon half over mussels. Cut remaining 1 lemon half into wedges for serving. Taste broth and season with kosher salt if needed.

Ladle mussels and broth among shallow bowls, then scatter reserved scallions over. Serve with country-style bread or steamed white rice if desired and lemon wedges for squeezing over.

Today, the monarch was given a tour of the Gold Museum in Bogotá, and later walked through the city’s main square, Plaza de Bolívar, with Mayor Carlos Fernando Galán.

The official visit runs through November 26, and the queen’s agenda includes learning about the country’s post-earthquake reconstruction efforts (three months after a 7.4-magnitude earthquake struck Colombia) and ongoing sustainability projects.

To arrive in the country, Mary went sharp in a black Forte Forte single-breasted blazer with a frilly sky-blue Stenströms Darya blouse, which she paired with black trousers, Malone Souliers Marion 70 pumps, and black Gucci glasses, plus some gold jewelry.

To round out the ensemble, she reached for sheer black tights, plus matching stilettos and a leather handbag.

Black-and-white heels added the perfect contrast to the colorful maxi.

#6767. Wide-Leg Jeans and Boots Are a Perfect Match—Here’s How to Style Them
