#6766. What Is Smart Aging? Experts Explain the Skin Care Shift Replacing Antiaging

Hailey Bieber’s preferred term reframes aging as prevention, protection, and smarter skin care.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Beauty terminology has evolved quite rapidly in recent years—particularly around aging. What was long labeled antiaging has shifted toward more realistic definitions of what happens to skin and the body as we get older. But the latest buzzword, longevity, as ubiquitous as it’s become, doesn’t exactly fill the gap for those seeking ways to keep skin, hair, and the body looking youthful. Hailey Bieber’s recent use of the term “smart aging” just may be the word substitute the category has been missing.

Natalia Guzman, FNP-C, CANS, a board-certified nurse practitioner at Atelier Aesthetix in Beverly Hills, explains that smart aging is a proactive, science-based approach to maintaining healthy, youthful-looking skin while embracing the natural aging process. “Rather than trying to ‘stop’ aging altogether, which is biologically impossible, smart aging focuses on understanding how our skin changes over time and making informed decisions to preserve a healthy, strong, overall youthful appearance,” Guzman tells ELLE.

From a dermatologic perspective, Dr. Brianna Olamiju, a board-certified dermatologist with Spring Street Dermatology in NYC, offers this explanation: “Smart aging is about being proactive, making informed skin care choices, and supporting the skin’s natural functions over time. It involves protecting the skin from environmental damage, maintaining a healthy skin barrier, stimulating collagen production when appropriate, and addressing concerns such as fine lines, wrinkles, uneven pigmentation, and loss of elasticity,” says Olamiju. Ultimately, she says, smart aging is about helping your skin look and feel its best at every stage of life while embracing the natural aging process.

Guzman emphasizes that aging beautifully isn’t about looking young. “It’s about looking and feeling like the best version of yourself at every stage of life.”

As skin evolves, collagen production gradually declines, cellular turnover slows, and environmental factors such as UV exposure, pollution, and lifestyle habits contribute to visible signs of aging. Guzman adds that smart aging means addressing these changes strategically through preventative skin care, healthy lifestyle choices, and, when appropriate, evidence-based aesthetic treatments. “I like to think of smart aging as investing in your skin’s future and being proactive rather than waiting until significant changes occur and attempting to reverse them,” Guzman says. “A lot of the time, the latter takes much more work, and sometimes it cannot be completely corrected.”

According to Guzman, the biggest difference between smart aging and traditional antiaging is the philosophy behind it. “For decades, the beauty industry has promoted the idea that aging is something we need to fight, prevent, or erase,” says Guzman. The term “antiaging” often focuses on correcting visible signs of aging, such as wrinkles, volume loss, pigmentation, and skin laxity.

Both Guzman and Olamiju agree that smart aging takes a more holistic, comprehensive, and realistic approach. Olamiju says that anti-aging creates the expectation that we should try to look younger indefinitely. “Smart aging shifts the focus toward skin health, prevention, and maintaining long-term skin quality. Rather than viewing wrinkles, fine lines, and changes in skin textureas something that must be eliminated, smart aging encourages us to support the skin as it naturally evolves. It is less about fighting aging and more about aging well,” she explains. This approach, she adds, also emphasizes consistency, prevention, and individualized treatments rather than chasing every new product or cosmetic trend.

Guzman adds that smart aging, instead of fighting age, means working with the skin’s biology to help it function and look its best for as long as possible. “The main difference is that we’re not simply chasing wrinkles. We're creating a long-term strategy that prioritizes skin quality, natural-looking results, and overall skin health,” Guzman emphasizes. “To me, anti-aging is often about correcting what we see today, while smart aging is about preventing and protecting what we’ll see tomorrow.”

Olamiju and Guzman are completely aligned on the first step in a smart aging routine: wearing sunscreen daily. “The foundation of smart aging starts with a consistent skincare routine and healthy lifestyle habits. Daily broad-spectrum sunscreen with an SPF of at least 30 is arguably the most important step because ultraviolet exposure is one of the leading contributors to premature skin aging, including wrinkles, loss of elasticity, and uneven pigmentation,” says Olamiju. Guzman adds that UV exposure contributes significantly to collagen breakdown and changes in skin texture, noting that sunscreen is essential even on cloudy days.

Olamiju also recommends incorporating topical antioxidants, such as vitamin C, to help protect against environmental oxidative stress, along with moisturizers containing ingredients like ceramides and hyaluronic acid to support hydration and the skin barrier. “Retinoids, including retinol and prescription tretinoin, can be beneficial for improving fine lines, skin texture, and collagen production, provided they are appropriate for the individual,” Olamiju describes.

Paula's Choice Resist Youth-Extending Daily Hydrating Fluid SPF 50

Sunday Riley Ceramor Ceramide Barrier Replenishing Cream

Bio-Therapeutic bt-sculpt Gen2 Microcurrent Facial Sculpting System

Once your at-home routine is in place, Guzman recommends strategically incorporating preventive and corrective aesthetic treatments. “Depending on the patient’s age, anatomy, and concerns, treatments such as neuromodulators, collagen-stimulating injectables, microneedling, and energy-based devices may help maintain or improve certain aspects of skin appearance,” says Guzman. The key, she adds, is choosing the right treatment for the right patient rather than following every new aesthetic trend. Olamiju includes chemical peels in a very similar list of in-office treatments, depending, of course, on an individual’s concerns, skin type, and goals.

Finally, the experts agree: don’t underestimate lifestyle. Adequate sleep, regular exercise, balanced nutrition rich in antioxidants and healthy fats, avoiding smoking, managing chronic stress, and limiting excessive alcohol consumption all contribute to overall health and can influence how our skin looks and functions.

The best time to start is earlier than most people think, Olamiju shares. Ideally in your 20s, when prevention can have a meaningful long-term impact. “This does not mean that someone in their 20s needs an elaborate skincare routine or aggressive cosmetic treatments. Rather, it is an opportunity to establish healthy habits, including daily sunscreen use, gentle cleansing, and consistent moisturizing,” says Olamiju.

Guzman advises starting a smart aging routine before you feel like you need to correct something, but it’s also never too late to begin. “I encourage patients to think of smart aging as something that evolves with them rather than a routine that begins at a specific birthday,” she adds, offering this decade-by-decade breakdown:

In your 20s. The priority should be prevention. This means establishing healthy skincare habits, wearing sunscreen consistently, maintaining hydration, and introducing targeted active ingredients when appropriate. At this stage, the goal isn’t to aggressively treat aging. It's to protect the skin and build a strong foundation.

In your 30s. You may begin noticing subtle changes in skin texture, fine lines, pigmentation, or elasticity. This is often a good time to reassess your skin care routine and consider professional treatments based on individual needs. It may also be time to consider a little Botox, energy-based treatments, and biostimulators, all depending on the individual’s needs.

In your 40s and 50s. Changes in collagen, facial volume, skin elasticity, and hormonal influences may become more noticeable. A smart aging approach at this stage may include a combination of medical-grade skin care, collagen-supporting treatments, and continued careful selection of aesthetic procedures.

In your 60s and beyond. The focus remains on maintaining skin health, supporting the skin barrier, addressing concerns such as dryness and uneven pigmentation, and choosing treatments that respect the skin’s changing needs.

Olamiju adds that even patients who begin prioritizing their skin health later in life can benefit significantly from consistent sun protection and an appropriately tailored skin care regimen.

Guzman says this is one of the most important conversations we should be having in modern aesthetics. Both experts say smart aging is not a one-size-fits-all concept. They explain that different skin types and skin tones can experience visible aging differently, and understanding those differences is essential to creating a safe and effective treatment plan.

Individuals with lighter skin tones may notice fine lines, redness, and visible sun damage earlier, particularly if they have a history of significant UV exposure. Those with darker skin tones may have more visible concerns related to uneven pigmentation, post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation, and changesin skin tone. “Melanin provides some natural protection against ultraviolet radiation, but it does not eliminate the risk of sun damage or skin cancer, so daily sunscreen remains essential for all skin tones,” Olamiju warns.

Guzman adds that these pigmentation concerns also become especially important when we introduce aesthetic treatments such as lasers, chemical peels, or microneedling. “Certain procedures and treatment settings carry a greater risk of pigmentation changes in patients with more melanin-rich skin, so proper patient selection, device choice, and clinical expertise are critical,” Guzman notes.

Overall, smart aging should combine science, prevention, consistency, and thoughtful aesthetic care. “Aging is inevitable. How we care for ourselves throughout that process is where we have the opportunity to make a meaningful difference,” Guzman concludes.

#6763. Alex Prager Has Hollywood Dreams

With a feature film and major solo shows, the artist is experiencing a moment like no other.

Alex Prager was in her cubicle at an internet startup, staring at the endless rows of other desks, when she realized that something was terribly wrong. “The rest of my life flashed in my mind,” she says. She began to cry. “It was like that Talking Heads song: And you may find yourself in a beautiful house / With a beautiful wife / And you may ask yourself, / ‘Well…how did I get here?’” she says. “This was not my life.” Soon after, she attended a show of William Eggleston’s work and left certain that her future was in photography. Within a week, she’d bought a used Nikon N90s camera and darkroom equipment. “I just knew, the way that you know you have a face and you know who you love. I knew from that moment forward what my path was,” she says.

It didn’t take long for Prager to start getting noticed in art circles. And this winter will be one of the most significant seasons of her career, with both a major solo museum show at the Crystal Bridges Museum of American Art in Bentonville, Arkansas, and an exhibition at the New York gallery Lehmann Maupin. DreamQuil, her first feature film, was released in theaters this fall and will be available to stream in December.

Prager’s color-saturated works capture cinematic scenes that bring to mind classic Hitchcock films. She creates full worlds for her photos, with sets, casts, and impeccable period costumes. Her pieces gained notice with her 2007 show “Polyester” at L.A.’s Robert Berman Gallery. The series showed women, perfectly attired in ’60s-era looks, posed in evocative ways. A heavily made-up girl clutches a Big Gulp, a flaxen braid thrown over one shoulder; four young women huddle together in swimsuits, a plane flying across the perfect blue sky above. An ominous feeling pervades.

Her work sparkles on the surface but explores something darker underneath. “The first color photograph I took was staged and a play on The Wizard of Oz and death, but it was very colorful and beautiful. I was interested in the way to explore something that felt layered. Underneath it, you find there are very uncomfortable things or darker stories and emotions,” Prager explains.

Prager steadily built up a following through a series of gallery shows, but things changed markedly in 2010 when her work was selected to be shown in the elite “New Photography” exhibit at MoMA. She had her first solo show at the Corcoran Gallery of Art in Washington, D.C., three years later. She’s also done work for magazines and brands like Cartier, Disney, and Apple.

“My work has always been about my personal challenges and things that I’m not really understanding in the world, but I think more and more it becomes less about me and my emotions. That’s probably also me becoming a mother and growing up, but the world has changed,” says Prager, whose son Francis is 9. The scale of her work has grown—bigger audiences, more ambitious concepts, a greater ability to stage elaborate photography—but the approach hasn’t. It’s still about the questions she needs answers to.

The idea for DreamQuil came to Prager during the pandemic (she was inspired by a meme that advertised YearQuil, a fictitious product that would allow people to sleep through the misery of 2020), and she wrote the script with her sister, Vanessa Prager. Elizabeth Banks stars as a woman who’s living in a depressing future world where people rarely leave their homes and finds herself dealing with her robot clone.

Opening two major shows within months of the release of her film wasn’t something Prager anticipated—each alone would have been a high point in her career—but she often does her best work amid a frenzy of activity. “I thrive in a bit of chaos,” she says. “I like to try to mold it and wrestle with it and attempt to control it and bend it to my will. It doesn’t always happen exactly the way I want, but it infuses me with inspiration and energy.”

This story appears in the December 2026/January 2027 issue of ELLE.

#6762. A New Azzedine Alaïa Retrospective Goes Beyond the Bandage Dress

The Fine Arts Museums of San Francisco examines the great couturier’s legacy

The bandage dress. The zipper dress. The perforated leather. The jersey hoods. The peplummed chemises. Azzedine Alaïa’s designs may not look so radical now, but almost 50 years ago, he changed the collective tide of fashion. The bandage dress is perhaps the most pervasive bit of his legacy, and it is emblematic of how the late French-Tunisian designer ushered in a new body-conscious silhouette in the ’80s, one made possible by technological innovations in materials that sculpted without any rigid, waist-cinching structure.

Alaïa’s designs “were completely shocking” to the public at the time, recounts Laura Camerlengo, curator in charge of costume and textile arts at the Fine Arts Museums of San Francisco. She has spent the past four years developing a new exhibition titled “Azzedine Alaïa: Sculpting Fashion,” the first major U.S. retrospective dedicated to the work of the famed couturier. It opened November 21.

Born in 1935 in Tunis, Alaïa first trained as a sculptor, a background evident in his body of work. In 1956, he decamped to Paris to pursue fashion full-time. After a brief internship at the house of Christian Dior, he worked under Guy Laroche for less than two years before taking on his own private clients, then launching his own atelier in the late ’70s. From there, he built the house we know today. His obsession with construction and material innovation allowed him to both celebrate and liberate the female form. He passed away in late 2017 at the age of 82.

Camerlengo first encountered Alaïa’s work as an assistant on the “Patrick Kelly Runway of Love” exhibition that the Philadelphia Museum of Art organized in 2014. “I was intrigued by how many of the new designers in Paris at this time were being supported by US consumers and retailers,” she says. After ascending to her new position in San Francisco years later, she cold-emailed Olivier Saillard, director of the Azzedine Alaïa Foundation in Paris. He was intrigued, and the two began working together on the exhibition almost immediately.

The exhibition focuses on 80 garments from across Alaïa’s celebrated career, divided over a series of archetypes—the bandage dresses, sculptures and shapes, black and white, silhouettes and suiting, and construction and craftsmanship—to explore how his design aesthetic evolved. Interspersed are designs worn by devotees like Tina Turner, Grace Jones, and Rihanna. One final gallery highlights his personal collections of the work of designers he most admired, like Christian Dior and Charles James.

Ahead, Camerlengo speaks to Bazaar about curating the new exhibition, contextualizing Alaïa’s work in today’s fashion landscape, and his enduring legacy.

The exhibition is not chronological. We look at a series of Alaïa’s archetypes like the bandage dress to explore how his design aesthetic evolved over the course of his career. He often himself said that he didn’t want to conform to seasonal trends; he was more interested in the evolution of his designs. And so the gallery shows a mix of objects dating from the 1980s to his final collection in 2017, and they’re largely grouped by type.

When you come in, you are greeted by Grace Jones, as we say, and the seminal purple hooded bandage dress from the fashion Oscars [a French event held in 1985]. As you move through, you’ll see a selection of the zipper dresses, the chiffon gowns, and other works that tie into some of the other themes. In each space, we mix the time periods. And that was something Alaïa himself often said. [He had a running joke that he didn’t know his own age.] He often said he was “as old as the pharaohs.” Basically, time was a construct for him.

That’s a great word for it. We could think of the popularity of the Japanese fashion designers who obscured women’s bodies with their voluminous styles. We could think of the suiting of the period. The 1970s is this period of workplace equality. Women are wearing suits in the style of masculine dress, but that also means they're obscuring their bodies. And then we can think of pop culture. I always think of Diane Keaton’s Annie Hall in 1977.

Some of the individuals I interviewed for my catalog text who were retailers in San Francisco at the time described Alaïa's designs as being like a whiplash. It was a complete 180 from the styles that they had gotten used to over the past decade, which also includes the rise of T-shirts and jeans for women as a mode of dress.

So here you have these very sexy, very form-fitting clothes. And part of the Alaïa aesthetic, as I see it, is the reclamation of the gaze. Rather than women being passively looked at—usually by men—they are instead commanding their own agency. As you said, it was completely earth-shattering and very shocking at the time.

Azzedine Alaïa was one of the few couturiers who could work completely by the methods of tailoring and by draping, start to finish, from the initial concept to the final product. I did not fully appreciate his tailoring and the suits he constructed beforehand. We have a selection of them in a gallery of the exhibition, and their construction is absolutely incredible. The way they fit the body, the way they uplift and empower women through cloth—again, very antithetical to what we saw in the decades prior in the 1970s. These are very form-fitting, tailored at the waist, but they have soft feminine touches as well that give you this interesting mix of femininity and strength at the same time.

Very important. We talk in the first gallery about how women, speaking generally, were the architects of his life. He learned to sew from his sister, who was working for a seamstress. He was encouraged by a local midwife in Tunisia to go to school at the Institute of Fine Arts. She helped him forge his papers to read 16—he was only 15 at the time. He learned his skills, not in formal training, but from women in Tunisia.

And then his early work was supported by a lot of leading figures of international society, including various Rothschilds, the French performer Arletty, the Swedish American actress Greta Garbo, and that's all before he even started to work formally under his own label in the 1980s. Of course, in the 1980s, he adopts Naomi Campbell, who becomes his protégé. He has close relationships with Grace Jones and Tina Turner. They were hugely important to him. In the exhibition, the clear invisible mounts that we use are actually based on the body of Naomi Campbell.

Probably the Grace Jones bandage dress from the fashion Oscars in 1985. Not only is it the progression of the bandage dress style that he experiments with throughout the course of the 1980s, but it represents a key moment in his career where he receives two very high accolades as part of the fashion Oscars and he’s recognized for his genius.

As far as the designs themselves, I think we would consider the bandage dress, for example, an iconic symbol of late 20th- and 21st-century fashion developed by Azzedine Alaïa, but of course iterated upon extensively. We've seen surges and resurgences in its popularity ever since.

And then as far as the landscape of the broader fashion system, Alaïa was someone who really eschewed seasonal calendars and trends. He was unconcerned with making things for the market. He often wanted to just produce clothes and share them when he felt they were ready. So I think his method of working foreshadows some of the conversations we’re having now about the overproduction of fashion and the overconsumption of fashion, even the detrimental environmental impacts that we see connected to it. He was really a forerunner, in a sense, to question the fashion system.

You can see how he really studied the construction of other garments and then adapted it in his own way in his designs. For example, he admired the work of Charles James, the great American fashion designer who was able to manipulate fabric to achieve weightless volume. And you see in Alaïa's own collections as he continues in his career, he does radial tucks and pleating to also get a certain amount of weightless volume in some of his gowns, very much in the spirit of James.

I hope our visitors appreciate the artistry of his work, first and foremost, and also the importance of handcraftsmanship. We are increasingly in an age of technology, especially with the rise of AI. What he created by his own hand is something that we can really marvel at.

#6760. Butternut Pasta With Sausage and Dates

Squash and sausage are a heroic autumnal pairing, especially in a weeknight-friendly pasta. Butternut is the easiest squash to source—not to mention easily found peeled and chopped (a blessing for those with more timid knife skills). But other winter squash like acorn or red kuri can stand in as desired. Whichever squash you select, a sharp, heavy chef’s knife is best for breaking down the chunky veg. But to make your life easier, microwave the squash for 1 to 2 minutes. It will slightly soften, making it simpler—and safer—to slice through.

Rigatoni’s ribbed surface and large holes help chunky squash sauce cling and bites of seared sausage and date wedge—opting for a nutty whole wheat variety will only add to this meal’s autumnal earthiness.

More cozy pastas this way: Balsamic Mushroom and Sausage Pasta; Creamy Red Curry Pasta; and Baked Feta Pasta With Green Sauce.

Cook 1 medium butternut squash (1½–2 lb.), peeled, halved, seeds removed, cut into 1" pieces (about 6 cups), in a large pot of boiling salted water until very tender, 12–14 minutes. Using a slotted spoon, transfer squash to a medium bowl. Reduce heat to low, cover pot, and leave on burner to keep warm.

Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large high-sided skillet or a medium Dutch oven over medium-high. Add 1 lb. hot or sweet Italian sausage, casings removed, and break up into small pieces with a wooden spoon. Cook, undisturbed, until deeply browned underneath, about 3 minutes. Continue to cook, stirring occasionally, until browned all over and just cooked through, 3–4 minutes more. Transfer to a plate with a slotted spoon.

Reduce heat to medium-low. Cook 3½ oz. Medjool dates (about 6), pitted, sliced lengthwise ¾" thick, in same pan, turning halfway though, until deeply browned, 3–4 minutes. Transfer to plate with sausage; season with salt.

Add remaining 3 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil to pan. Add 6 garlic cloves, thinly sliced, ½ tsp. freshly ground pepper, ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and reserved squash. Cook, scraping up browned bits and smashing squash with spoon to break up, until lightly browned and broken up into a chunky paste (pieces do not need to be even), about 5 minutes.

Meanwhile, return cooking liquid in reserved pot to a boil over high heat. Cook 12 oz. short tube pasta (such as rigatoni) until very al dente, about 3 minutes less than package directions (it will finish cooking in sauce). Drain, reserving 3 cups pasta cooking liquid.

Add 1 cup pasta cooking liquid to squash and break up squash with spoon until a saucy consistency. Add pasta, another 1 cup pasta cooking liquid, and reserved sausage and dates. Increase heat to medium-high; cook, adding more pasta cooking liquid as needed, until pasta is al dente and coated in sauce, 2–3 minutes. Remove from heat and stir in 1 Tbsp. sherry vinegar or balsamic vinegar. Taste pasta and add more vinegar, salt and/or pepper if needed.

#6755. Creamy Miso Mushroom Pasta

Mushroom fans: Bookmark this recipe immediately. It will give you everything you want when you crave a creamy mushroom pasta as soon as the crisp fall air sets in. The hardworking trio of miso, Parmesan, and touch of soy sauce delivers an umami bomb of flavor that doesn’t overwhelm, but magnifies, the best of earthy mushrooms. Coupled with luscious noodles enrobed in a creamy (but not heavy) sauce, what’s not to love?

A few helpful notes for pasta success: (1) While any pasta will work, egg-based noodles, like the fettuccine I recommend here, are the best complement to the sauce. The nests (Rummo and De Cecco are great accessible brands) cook far faster than standard straight pasta, almost as fast as fresh. (2) Don’t be afraid to season your mushrooms! Yes, I know this goes against all that we’ve learned about salting them too early in the cooking process, but just a bit of salt will get them going and releasing their water faster for eventual crisp-browned edges. Don’t be alarmed if you see a pool of water at the start of the mushroom cooking—that’s to be expected and just keep going until it evaporates. (3) Don’t forget to reserve plenty of pasta cooking liquid, which is critical to the eventual sauce’s success. I prefer transferring the al dente noodles from the pasta pot to the skillet, letting any residual water come with it (and to ensure you don’t accidentally drain away that liquid gold).

Find more fall favorite meals here: Weeknight Pumpkin Chili; Cream-ish of Mushroom Soup; Mushrooms au Poivre.

Cook 12 oz. egg-based pasta (such as fettuccine nests) in a large pot of boiling salted water, stirring occasionally, until al dente, about 1 minute less than package directions.

Meanwhile, heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large skillet over high. Add 1 lb. crimini or mixed mushrooms (such as button, maitake, oyster, and/or shiitake), sliced ¼" thick or torn into bite-size pieces, and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt and toss to coat evenly. Spread out into an even layer and cook, undisturbed, until mushrooms are starting to release their liquid, 2–3 minutes (do not be alarmed; there will be a lot). Continue to cook, tossing occasionally, until moisture is almost completely evaporated and edges of mushrooms are starting to brown, 6–8 minutes.

Reduce heat to medium-high; add 5 garlic cloves, thinly sliced, and remaining 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil. Cook, tossing often, until garlic is fragrant and golden, 1–2 minutes. Pour in ¾ cup dry white wine, scraping up any browned bits stuck to bottom of pan with a sturdy spatula or flat-edged wooden spoon, and cook until wine is mostly evaporated (you should have about 1 Tbsp. liquid left in pan), about 4 minutes. Reduce heat to medium-low. Add 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, cut into pieces, and 2 Tbsp. white miso and stir until melted and combined. Slowly pour in ½ cup heavy cream, scraping up any browned bits stuck to bottom of pan; simmer until thickened slightly, 2–3 minutes. Add 1 Tbsp. low-sodium soy sauce; season with freshly ground pepper. Taste and add more salt if needed.

Using tongs, transfer pasta to mushroom mixture and add 1 cup pasta cooking liquid. Increase heat to medium and bring to a simmer. Cook, tossing constantly and adding ½–1 cup more pasta cooking liquid to reach desired sauce consistency if needed, until pasta is al dente and liquid has thickened slightly, about 3 minutes.

Remove pan from heat. Add 1 oz. Parmesan, finely grated (about ½ cup) to pasta and toss to combine. Season generously with more pepper; taste and season with more salt if needed.

Divide pasta among shallow bowls and top with more Parmesan and pepper.

#6752. Cider-Braised Sausage and Lentils

I haven’t made a meal in the French countryside in fall; but sometimes I dream, and these slow-cooked lentils are what I’d make in such a situation. Back in my American reality, it still makes an excellent dinner or hearty weekend lunch. Look for mild smoked Austrian or German sausages, which lean on mellow herbs and warm spices (sometimes, if you’re lucky, they’re also stuffed with a nutty cheese)—this style is already cooked to a safe temperature, but the casing’s texture is greatly improved when seared before reheating. If your shop only carries sweet Italian sausage or something like an apple-stuffed chicken sausage, both work in a pinch. What a treat it will be to actually finish off that gallon of apple cider you impulsively bought last week in the lentil-braising liquid.

More great fall recipes this way: Charred Cabbage With Sausages and Apples; Pork Chops With Green Chiles and Apples; Mustardy Cider-Braised Pork Chops.

Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large high-sided skillet or a medium heavy pot over medium-high. Cook 6–8 smoked Austrian or German sausages (1–1½ lb.), turning often, until deeply browned on all sides, about 4 minutes. Transfer to a plate.

Combine 1 large red or yellow onion, finely chopped, 3 celery stalks, finely chopped, 1 large carrot, peeled, finely chopped, 1 tsp. freshly ground pepper, and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and remaining 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in same pan. Reduce heat to medium and cook, stirring often and scraping up any browned bits stuck to bottom of pan, until vegetables are softened, 6–8 minutes. Add 5 garlic cloves, thinly sliced, and cook, stirring, just until fragrant, about 1 minute.

Add 2 cups low-sodium chicken or vegetable broth, 1½ cups apple cider, 1½ cups French green lentils, picked through, rinsed, ½ cup finely chopped parsley, 3 Tbsp. Dijon or whole grain mustard, and remaining ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt to pan. Increase heat to high and bring to a boil. Cook until liquid is slightly reduced, about 5 minutes. Cover pan, reduce heat to medium-low, and cook until lentils are tender and most of the liquid is absorbed but mixture is still saucy, 30–35 minutes.

Uncover pan and stir in 1 Tbsp. apple cider vinegar or red wine vinegar. Nestle sausages into lentil mixture and add any juices pooled on plate. Simmer until sausages are warmed through, about 5 minutes. Top with parsley leaves and reserved celery leaves to serve.

#6749. The Opera Coat Is Everywhere—Here’s the Chinese Influence Behind the Trend

A moment to appreciate the history of the trending piece.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s been living rent-free in my mind for the better part of this year: Conner Ives’s silk brocade opera coat, which captured nearly every fashion lover’s heart when it debuted on the fall/winter 2026 runway at London Fashion Week. Reminiscent of Mary-Kate Olsen’s fur-trimmed Balmain coat from the 2013 Met Gala, this particular stunner, along with the designer’s other iterations, seems to be what every It girl is wearing.

The coat—crafted with silk tapestries dating back to the Qing dynasty and with hand-embroidered depictions of red-crowned cranes, bamboo leaves, and flowers—was undoubtedly the first wave in what would become a tsunami of a trend: opera coats. And while plenty of faux fur-trimmed coats have made their way onto the market, those featuring Chinese imagery like that on Ives’s iterations have taken center stage.

“There’s been a real change in how people perceive Chinese design,” Cheryl Leung, founder and creative director of Sau Lee, tells ELLE. Leung attributes the opera coat’s rise in popularity to fashion’s shift away from minimalism and consumers’ growing appetite for expressive pieces, richer fabrics, and textures. “Brocade is a great example because it has so much character,” she says. “The fabrics can be quite ornate, but when you put them into a modern silhouette or style them with something very simple, they feel completely different.”

Chinese design has often been presented through a Western lens, but designers like Leung and Kim Shui are creating work rooted in their own cultural perspectives. Shui, the Chinese-American, New York-based creative director behind her eponymous label, has long been drawn to Chinese garments and the ways they communicate identity. “I’m particularly interested in the visual language and craftsmanship of traditional Chinese garments, whether it’s the silhouette of the qipao (a traditional Chinese dress dating back to the Qing dynasty), the richness of brocade, or traditional techniques like embroidery and intricate knot closures,” Shui tells ELLE. “I look at these elements not as things to reproduce literally, but as a vocabulary that I can reinterpret.”

While the trend has gained widespread popularity, the Chinese design details behind it can sometimes be overlooked. “I’ve seen so many ‘trending winter coat’ videos featuring Chinese brocade coats with fur trim and traditional Chinese embroidered silk coats, but they don’t make any reference to their Chinese origins,” Shui says. “Instead of using terms like ‘qipao’ or ‘mandarin collar,’ creators will often describe these details generically as a ‘stand collar’ or ‘high neckline.’ It’s important to acknowledge where these design elements come from and the history behind them—why erase the cultural context?”

Leung sees the coat’s popularity as an opportunity to bring attention to its history and the designers reinterpreting it. Shui and her team have also been creating content about the histories of brocade, embroidery, and the qipao, as well as the cultural references behind her collections to bring more awareness of the craft to her audience.

“I do think there’s a difference between appreciating a design tradition and overlooking where it came from,” Leung says. “If a designer has been inspired by Chinese textiles, craftsmanship, or traditional clothing, acknowledging that doesn’t take anything away from their creativity. If anything, it gives the work more depth and helps people appreciate the original references.” Shui adds that brands outside the culture have a “responsibility to acknowledge their origins rather than presenting them as an entirely new aesthetic.”

Fashion has been both a vehicle for cultural exchange and a product of it—and trends will undoubtedly come and go. Not everyone will be interested in the cultural history of the piece they buy—and Leung doesn’t necessarily think they need to be. Shui is happy to see people from all backgrounds embrace garments from outside of their own cultures. “I hope this renewed interest encourages people to look beyond the garment itself and discover the rich traditions and craftsmanship behind it,” she says.

#6744. Amanda Seyfried Makes the Case for Fall Florals in a Sheer Carolina Herrera Dress

The actor joined Jimmy Fallon in an embellished runway piece

Florals... for fall? While not quite groundbreaking, per say, the pattern is certainly not as common during the chilly months as it might be in the springtime. But leave it to Amanda Seyfried to show us how it’s done.

For an appearance on The Tonight Show Starring Jimmy Fallon, the actor wore a black Carolina Herrera midi dress that, despite being from Wes Gordon’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection, felt like an appropriate choice for November.

Covered in 3D flowers, the sleeveless number played with texture and depth. It was crafted from a sheer mesh base and stitched with black flower-shaped crochet appliqués, each adorned with a cluster of small snow-colored beads that brought in a bit of winter white.

Seyfried paired the embroidered dress with shimmering Christian Louboutin Miss Z pumps, crafted with a similar sheer base and embellished with crystals and black beads. The signature red sole gave the look an extra holiday-ready touch, and the star finished things off with a pair of diamond studs from Tiffany & Co.

The Mamma Mia! actor joined Fallon for a conversation about how she got involved in Nathan Fielder’s Elizabeth Holmes documentary, You Can See Everything. “It was never my intention to recreate Elizabeth Holmes ever again,” Seyfried, who previously portrayed the Theranos founder in biopic series The Dropout, told the host. “But Nathan Fielder, the director, reached out and said, ‘Hey, I’d love to pick your brain.’”

Continuing, she revealed that the two talked on FaceTime for an hour, before Fielder asked if she would be comfortable being filmed watching the footage. “A couple weeks later, I was watching the footage with four cameras in my apartment,” she revealed. “I was like, ‘How did I get here?’”

#6737. Satin Outfits Look Even Better With Cozy Winter Textures

Cozy knits, wool coats, and faux fur make satin feel winter-ready.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There was a time when satin seemed reserved for summer evenings or special occasions, but that has changed. Nowadays, the silky fabric is being paired with richer textures for everyday fall and winter outfits, not just for parties.

It all comes down to styling, and it’s a lesson in contrasts. Fall and winter are all about texture, so teaming a satin skirt with a soft cashmere knit keeps the look sleek and sophisticated while bringing a cozier feel to daytime. For evening events, silk on silk is a natural choice, and you can always add a faux-fur scarf or stole to embrace the cold-weather spirit.

Color plays its part, too. Swap summer’s pastels for deeper, moodier shades such as chocolate brown, bottle green, burgundy, and navy, which lend satin a richer, more seasonal feel. Champagne and ivory remain timeless, especially when worn head to toe beneath a tonal wool coat.

Whether you’re dressing for a holiday party, a workday, or a weekend brunch, below we’ve rounded up some inspo for how to style satin this season.

#6733. Long “Squoval” Nails Are the Elegant Manicure Shape Taking Over This Winter: 5 Designs to Try Now

The celebrity-approved nail shape pairs perfectly with everything from soft neutrals to dark, festive shades

If you’ve found yourself torn between square and oval nails this winter, consider the celebrity-approved “squoval” shape. Practical and elegant, the hybrid silhouette has grown in popularity thanks to its effortless look that elongates the nails while staying durable for everyday wear.

Most recently, Zendaya, Penélope Cruz, and Florence Pugh were spotted at the 17th annual Governors Awards, all opting for a soft milky nude finish in the flattering nail shape. "What makes this manicure so beautiful is its simplicity. The long squoval shape paired with a soft, sheer pink polish creates a really understated look," says Susan Carroll, founder of Notting Hill beauty destination Young LDN. "We’re seeing a continued move towards refined, natural-looking manicures that feel luxurious without needing intricate nail art. This is the perfect example of that quiet luxury aesthetic, where the shape, finish, and attention to detail really do all the talking."

To recreate the look, Carroll recommends asking your nail technician for a long squoval shape with softly rounded corners. "The key is ensuring the sides remain relatively straight while the edges are gently softened. Regular cuticle oil and maintenance appointments are essential, particularly with longer nails, to keep the manicure looking healthy, elegant, and perfectly shaped," she adds.

And though celebrities have been leaning on glossy, sheer nude finishes, plenty of hues and nail designs are great for experimenting with the silhouette. Ahead, five ideas to bookmark for your next appointment.

Soft, versatile, and endlessly wearable, a high-shine soft pink in a long squoval shape is hard to beat.

Feeling festive? A classic shade of red can instantly add a chic pop of color to any holiday look.

#6731. The Launch: November’s Must-Know Fashion News

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fall is in full swing, and with it, so is the fashion industry. As the leaves continue to turn and preparations for the holiday season get serious, brands are finding new ways to celebrate with exciting new capsules, collaborations, and stocking-worthy releases. Look no further than J.J. Martin, whose new book, Table Energy, explores the art of Italian entertaining, and the Rimowa x Maison Margiela collab, which turns travel into something even more desirable. Plus, there’s plenty of reason to get out and shop, with the likes of L/Uniform, Danielle Frankel, and Bulgari all opening new storefronts. Below, all the fashion news to know this month.

Where: Online at moncler.com, rickowens.eu, select Moncler stores, and Rick Owens flagship stores

Why: Moncler and Rick Owens return to their shared winter universe with a collection that makes outerwear the main event. Quilted jackets arrive in oversized shapes, punctuated by metal studs, utility pockets, and a strap that lets you carry them when the layers come off. The palette stays pared back—black, silver-toned Palladium, Cloud Grey, and Grease—with matching boots and sunglasses to finish the look. In a black-and-white campaign photographed by Owens, screen legend Joe Dallesandro, known for his turn in Andy Warhol’s Superstars, brings his own countercultural history to the collection. “Little Joe mythologized an indifferent beauty in Lou Reed’s hymn to queer subculture,” Owens said in a statement. “‘Take a Walk on the Wild Side’ was a turning point for me and a lot of other suppressed kids of my generation. his inclusion in Avedon’s photos of Warhol's entourage illustrated the magic of finding one’s tribe, for better or worse.”

Moncler + Rick Owens Combo Flight Down Bomber Jacket

Moncler + Rick Owens Cotton Blend Cargo Skirt

Moncler + Rick Owens Trailgrip Pentalace Ankle Boots

Where: Online at gap.com and in select Gap stores

Why: Zac Posen is keeping things simple this winter season—and the latest GapStudio wardrobe is edited to just 12 pieces. The designer’s capsule gives familiar staples a sharper feel through sleek silhouettes, precise tailoring, and touches of unexpected texture like a playful teddy bear coat rendered in a cozy caramel fabric. Other highlights include elevated essentials that Posen has prioritized at the brand: a turtleneck maxi dress, a sleek poplin shirt, and high-rise seamed stirrup jeans. “For winter 2026, we wanted to move beyond traditional occasion wear with versatile pieces designed to be worn from day to night,” Posen said of the collection, which came to life on singer EJAE in the accompanying campaign, in a statement. “There’s still that level of drama with thoughtful tailoring and exaggerated lines, but it’s balanced by a focused palette that feels elegant and wearable.”

Why: Parisian leather goods label L/Uniform is making its New York debut with a flagship on the Upper East Side. Following boutiques in Paris and Tokyo, the store marks the brand’s first American address with founder Jeanne Signoles working with architect Jakob Sprenger to create a space that balances the polish of a boutique with the practical spirit at the heart of the label. “Opening a store in New York was a priority for me from the very beginning,” Signoles says. “I always felt that L/Uniform naturally belonged here, with the city’s energy, its independence, and its very strong connection to everyday life. Our bags are made to be used—to work, to travel, to accompany daily life.”

Why: A historic Tribeca landmark is the setting for Danielle Frankel’s new flagship. At 6 Harrison Street, the bridal house has created a 12,000-square-foot space where original architectural character meets a considered, contemporary interior by Augusta Hoffman Studio. Private rooms make space for a more personal appointment, while the wider setting invites visitors to explore the collection at ease.

Danielle Frankel Chantilly Lace Degradé Border Drop Veil

What: High jewelry collection and new boutique

Where: 237 Worth Avenue, Palm Beach, Florida

Why: A 1920s Palm Beach estate is a fitting setting for Bulgari’s first U.S. presentation of “Eclettica.” Designed by Addison Mizner, the waterfront home hosted a gala that also marked the opening of the house’s new boutique. Venus Williams, Chanel Iman, and Becca Bloom were among the guests, with Jewel performing. The collection includes 15 transformable designs—the most Bulgari has presented in a single collection—alongside more than 50 high jewelry pieces. With its Roman roots and sculptural approach to high jewelry, Bulgari’s “Eclettica” brings a distinctly Italian point of view to Palm Beach.

Where: Online at rimowa.com and maisonmargiela.com and select Rimowa and Maison Margiela stores

Why: Some things look better after they’ve been lived in. That idea is at the heart of Rimowa and Maison Margiela’s new travel collaboration, which brings together two signatures: the German luggage house’s familiar grooves and Margiela’s hand-painted white Bianchetto finish. Designed to change with use, the suitcase and matching bag will take on marks and patina as they travel. A campaign by Dexter Lander brings that idea to life in a Haussmannian apartment, where the white paint seems to spill beyond the pieces themselves.

What: Bird on a Rock installation, window displays, and holiday décor

Where: The Landmark; 727 5th Avenue, New York

Why: Tiffany & Co. has switched on the holiday season at The Landmark, with Amanda Seyfried leading the way. The actress lit the flagship’s Bird on a Rock installation by placing an ornament on its Blue Box tree, marking the third year of the display and a full-circle moment. Seyfried stars in the house’s new holiday film, “You & Me,” which travels between present-day New York and a memory from the ’90s. Mikey Madison, Jordyn Woods, and Louisa Jacobson joined the celebration.

Tiffany & Co. Bird on a Rock Wings Narrow Ring

Why: Just in time for the holidays, La DoubleJ founder J.J. Martin has released Table Energy, a new book about the art of Italian entertaining, with a foreword by fashion writer Leandra Medine Cohen. Its pages move from alfresco lunches to candlelit dinners, all in the colorful, convivial spirit of La DoubleJ. The book made its debut at the brand’s Upper East Side boutique, The Lighthouse, where Martin, ELLE Decor’s head of editorial content Elisa Lipsky-Karasz, and ELLE editor-in-chief Nina Garcia gathered to celebrate l’art de la table.

Where: Online at tecovas.com, aninebing.com, and select retail locations

Why: Anine Bing has a new way to kick up a look. The designer has teamed up with Tecovas on a capsule that reworks the label’s Loretta and Jamie boots with star embroidery and polished goat leather. Cowboy hats and belts round out the lineup, which launches alongside Anine Bing’s spring/summer 2027 collection.

Tecovas Anine Bing x Tecovas Plaque Buckle Belt

#6725. 7 Sneaker Outfits That Put a Fresh Spin on Your Favorite Kicks

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I’m what you might call a sneaker hoarder. But after building a capsule wardrobe I currently swear by, I’ve found that three pairs have become the foundation of most of my fall outfits: my black Adidas Superstars, which tap into the retro sneaker revival and rising noir sneaker trend I’m loving lately; my earth-toned Salomon XT-Whispers, perfect for an easy gorpcore moment; and my lilac Adidas x Wales Bonner Japan shoes, which add a subtle splash of color to neutral looks while complementing the rich hues in my autumn lineup. With those themes in mind, and inspired by some of this year’s most viral celebrity getups and standout street-style looks, I’ve gathered a quick guide to seven exciting sneaker outfits below.

Maybe you’re eager to invest in a new staple pair, or maybe you already have one of these favorites tucked away at the back of your closet. Either way, there’s plenty of fresh inspiration to copy and paste this season. Ahead, discover key pairs to shop (if you don’t already own them) and the chic pieces to wear with them.

Y2K icons like Vans and Converse are quietly making a comeback, stylishly co-signed by stars like Bella Hadid and Margot Robbie. Here, Robbie nails the cold-weather off-duty formula, swapping classic booties for black All Star high-tops to give her cozy gray polo, leather trench, and straight-leg jeans a more casual edge. A zesty red beanie completes the look, breaking up the muted tones.

By Anthropologie 100 Percent Brushed Cashmere Beanie

Even a few years after Adidas Sambas surged in popularity, the legendary indoor soccer-inspired shoe has cemented its status as a bona fide modern classic. Zoë Kravitz was recently spotted wearing a black-and-white pair while filming her new rom-com, Die Alive, in New York, styling them with flowy black trousers and a burgundy sweater and scarf. It’s a polished yet easygoing ensemble perfect for everything from bakery runs to overdue weekend catch-ups.

Saks Basic Cashmere Easy Crewneck Sweater

Hollister High-Rise Pleated Wide-Leg Pants

Or, as aforementioned, try switching up a neutral outfit with a colorful pair of kicks. As Jennifer Lawrence shows, an emerald- or forest-green sneaker feels just right for fall and winter, easily styled back to baggy jeans, a chunky turtleneck, and a navy coat.

The coolest outfits often hinge on contrast, mixing different textures, proportions, and aesthetics. This Paris Fashion Week showgoer went polished on top with a striped button-down and plaid blazer (also creating a nice pattern play) and sporty on bottom with classic track pants and chunky black Asics sneakers. An oversized denim tote introduces yet another interesting element.

Favorite Daughter The Dean Plaid Notch Lapel Blazer

Speaking of contrast, I love the idea of using a chunky sneaker to give tailored separates a cool, nonchalant feel. A relaxed-fit tuxedo blazer and drapey wide-leg trousers are a match made in street-style heaven when teamed with a boxy white tee and metallic trainers, like the celebrity-favorite New Balance 530s.

Banana Republic Factory Double Breasted Tuxedo Blazer

Lauren Ralph Lauren Pleated Stretch Jersey Wide-Leg Pant

PerfectWhiteTee Harley Cotton Boxy Crew Tee

Retro runner silhouettes, with their slim, streamlined uppers and slightly thicker soles, are another IYKYK trend to keep on your radar. Taking a cue from this Paris Fashion Week attendee, don’t be afraid to work traditionally spring-ready colors into your look: a baby-blue cable-knit sweater and soft yellow track jacket look lovely—and still feel relevant—alongside a chocolate-brown maxi skirt, matching sneakers, and a rich burgundy handbag.

Vineyard Vines Cashmere Cable Crewneck Sweater

Just like a go-to pair of low-profile kicks, a good ol’ graphic tee can be styled with just about anything in your closet—and it’s the kind of playful, sentimental piece that’ll spark joy every time you reach for it. For a unique combo, pair your favorite artist tee with sporty pieces like a nylon skirt and track jacket. An oversized briefcase-style bag adds a sleek touch.

#6724. Your Scorpio Monthly Horoscope for December 2026

Your time is precious, Scorpio, how do you want to wield it?

You’re focused on how you invest your energy, Scorpio. The month begins with the Sun traveling through Sagittarius, bringing attention to your resources. You’re more aware of how limited your time is and want to use it toward something meaningful. This can be a season of making investments—whehther it’s a new idea, signing up for a class that can help you down the line, or making an important purchase. You can also gain more attention at work and stand out for your gifts and expertise.

You’re drawing in more attention once Venus enters your sign on December 4! This is your season to take up space and let others be infatuated by your mysterious aura. You might focus more on how you look, changing up your sense of style and feeling more confident when you choose to self-express authentically. This can also bring support into your world—new relationships or opportunities that stir up passion, creativity, and pleasure in your world.

There’s a new money-making idea you’re ready to explore once Mercury moves into Sagittarius on December 6. You might want to apply to a different type of job or ask your boss for a raise. You’re also thinking more about your finances and how to get your money in order. This energy can also help you invest in yourself and even some of your most outlandish ideas so you can bet on yourself and bring some of your dreams to life.

You might start a new job or make an impromptu purchase under the New Moon in Sagittarius on December 8. This energy can also help you quit a job or contract that’s felt limiting or constrictive. You might also make an important purchase that brings a sense of freedom into your daily life. This energy also helps you stand up for what you believe in and can help you reflect on your relationship with your personal values. Could you write a list of them if someone asked you? That’s a great exercise to try under these moonbeams.

It’s time to clean up your act when Saturn goes direct in Aries on December 10. You’re very well aware of certain habits and patterns you need to break and what you need to replace them with. If you’ve been in a season of saying “yes” to everyone else at your own expense, this can help you firm up on your boundaries. This is also a reminder to tend to your mind, body, and spirit—you have to put your oxygen mask on first before you can help others.

Asking for help can bring the relief you so desperately need when Neptune stations direct in Aries on December 12. This is your reminder that it never hurts to ask for help when you need it. You might also want to incorporate more mystical or creative elements into your daily routine if life is starting to feel too rigid and bland.

You’re making an important decision about your life path and direction when Jupiter stations retrograde in Leo, also on December 12. You could quit a job, change career paths, or accept a role that requires an adjustment period. You’re also standing out for your good nature, talent, and generosity. You could receive positive compliments or feedback and your lesson here is to soak up the praise!

The Sun enters Capricorn and the Winter Solstice takes place on December 21, bringing important news your way. You might finally express something important to someone or perhaps you learn a secret that brings new understanding to a situation. You’re also in a more curious mood and this season helps you commit to studying subjects and learning skills that have always interested you. You have the dedication to stay the course.

A Full Moon in Cancer on December 23 helps you speak your truth. An ongoing conversation can reach a boiling point or new, critical information is revealed. This can also look like learning a piece of information that you can’t unlearn—that also changes your perspective in your personal life or worldview. If you’ve been studying something this can mark a moment of proficiency, gaining recognition, or getting something published. Traveling can also bring ah-hah moments that shake up what you thought was once possible.

The month wraps up with Mercury moving into Capricorn on December 25, helping you to commit to what piques your interest over the next few weeks. You might sign up for a workshop online or try out new events in your neighborhood or community. This is also lovely energy for brushing up on your own divination and spiritual practices. Connecting with your Ancestors and ancestral spiritual practices can bring the guidance you’ve been seeking.

#6723. The Best Thing to Buy This Black Friday? Diamonds

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I know it’s a tsunami of Black Friday and Cyber Monday content out there. Trust me when I say: I’m as inundated by it as you are. That said, it’s literally my job to know what is and is not worth shopping. This year, Brilliant Earth has one of the best deals I’ve ever seen—free diamonds with any purchase.

It’s actually called the brand’s Black Friday Diamond Giveaway. Yes, “giveaway,” which is indeed different from “sale.” As in, you can get diamonds for free. Well, nearly free.

Today through November 28, use code BLACKFRIDAY to receive a free diamond pendant necklace on any purchase under $1,000. To be clear, that means you can spend any amount of money under $1,000 and get free diamonds. Got it? Just want to make sure we’re on the same page here.

Brilliant Earth Lab Diamond Tube Huggie Earrings

Brilliant Earth Allegra Baguette and Round Lab Diamond Tennis Bracelet

Brilliant Earth Elizabeth Taylor Lab Diamond Drop Earrings

Brilliant Earth Medium Perfect Open Hoop Earrings

Brilliant Earth Baguette and Round Lab Diamond Crossover Ring

Brilliant Earth Emerald Cut Lab Diamond Riviere Necklace

Brilliant Earth Perfect Martini Lab Diamond Stud Earrings

Brilliant Earth Emerald Cut Lab Diamond Bezel Solitaire Pendant

Purchases over $1,000 can use the same code to receive free 1-carat lab-grown diamond studs. For shoppers making purchases over $3,000, the code will get you both 1-carat diamond studs and a 1-carat lab-grown diamond necklace.

For Cyber Monday, the offer remains the same, but the code changes. From November 29 to 30, use code CYBERMONDAY to earn your free diamond jewelry.

Do I even need to hammer home the point about what this means for your gift-giving possibilities? You can get a two-for-one value on presents for the jewelry lovers in your life. And who doesn’t want diamonds this Christmas?

#6722. The Gap Black Friday Sale Is a Holiday Miracle

The Gap Black Friday Sale kills two birds with one stone. The stone? Up to 60% off sitewide. The birds? Gifts for loved ones and gifts for you. Since its founding during the Summer of Love in the epicenter of the hippie revolution, the American fashion label has outfitted generations with stylish clothes both timely and timeless. And while it’s come a long way since its early days slinging Levi’s jeans and vinyl records, Gap’s designs are as relevant now as they were during Haight & Ashbury’s heyday. The prices, too, have remained accessible for the average American, even with inflation going insane.

Gap’s recent seasons have put itself back in the limelight, too, with the recent appointment of creative director Zac Posen as well as a string of killer collaborations and good ol’ fashioned Gap ads. Now that the best Black Friday clothing deals are here, you can score cartfuls of great menswear from hard-wearing denim to baby-soft cashmere sweaters—all for up to 60% off. But enough chatter. Here are just a handful of our favorites.

A lot of printed fleece can look a bit corny, IMO, but the fact that this one is a medium-pile with a very chill plaid in a good colorway makes me reconsider my stance.

Thermal tees are an easy hack for adding texture and warmth to your fit without getting too bulky. Extra points if it comes in a cool color, too.

Alright, alright, the Gap logo beanie is actually pretty cool.

This plus a beefy, broken-in hoodie, patched-up jeans, and heavy boots make for a 10/10 winter outfit.

Gap's main forte, in my opinion, is denim. Makes sense, since that's what the brand originally opened with. These heavyweight jeans are actually heavy and feel satisfyingly stiff, like a solid pair of Japanese denim for a fraction of the price.

If my math is correct, this is a great deal. And here's my work to prove it: $23 dupe > $5000 original.

Save for Supreme, few graphic hoodies have gone on to icon status like Gap's logo hoodie.

Perfect for layering over (or under!) everything this season.

Chunky-gauge rib knit, a proper tall zip-up neck, and a relaxed silhouette make this quarter-zip the stuff of '90s heartthrobs.

The polo cardigan is one of the great menswear hybrids that does everything well and this one in particular does it better than most.

The best things to buy at Gap? Start with the hits. The brand has been on a heater lately, mining its deep archive for all-star classics while dialing in modern silhouettes and workwear staples. Outerwear (especially the lighter layers), pants, shirts, sweaters, and sweats are all strong plays—especially if you haven’t checked in for a while.

This year in the US, Black Friday is on Friday, November 28, a day after Thanksgiving. While that’s the official date, many retailers roll out deals throughout November. You can expect the biggest discounts to happen between Thanksgiving and Cyber Monday, but be on the look out for retailers to launch sales early.

Cyber Monday lands on December 1 this year, the Monday after Thanksgiving. Most brands keep the deals rolling through the week—but don’t bank on it.

#6720. Your Sagittarius Monthly Horoscope for December 2026

Happy birthday, Sagittarius! The month starts out with the Sun moving through your sign, asking you to take up space! This is a season of new beginnings—from new personal goals to new relationships entering your life. You can use this momentum however you’d like, and you’re bursting with the confidence necessary to charge forward boldly with excitement. You might also want to play around with your self-expression and style, from posting sexy selfies to finally getting a big and bold new tattoo. It’s time to try something new!

You’re a bit more nostalgic when it comes to love once Venus enters Scorpio on December 4. You might find yourself fantasizing about ex-lovers or even glossing over the bad and romanticizing the past in some way. This season can also remind you how much you benefit from spending some time alone every now and then. Slowing down and reconnecting with yourself will help strengthen your relationship with your inner voice and intuition, as well.

You’re learning important, personal news once Mercury enters your sign on December 6. You could have a bright idea or new goal you want to set for yourself. This also puts you in a mindset to focus on yourself and your needs, which can bring about progress on your personal goals. You might also have important personal news you want to share with others, letting people catch a glimpse of who you really are and what matters most to you.

A New Moon, in your sign, on December 8 helps you recommit to yourself. This is your yearly permission slip to forgive yourself, release old baggage, and show up differently. You’re ready to show up more authentically by focusing on the people, projects, and hobbies that light you up. Who and what makes you feel most like yourself? Spend more time investing in those areas of your life. This energy also brings new beginnings into your world, and you can use this momentum however you see fit.

You’re setting firm boundaries in your romantic life when Saturn stations direct in Aries on December 10. You might need to end a fling or address an uncomfortable topic. This can also give you the push you need to make a commitment and go all-in. You’re also feeling ready to stick with a certain hobby or project and take it more seriously. You have the structure necessary to pour consistent effort into your vision or whatever brings you joy.

The creative inspiration you’ve been waiting for arrives when Neptune stations direct in Aries on December 12. If you’ve felt stuck dealing with writer’s block or some form of stagnant creative energy, that starts to subside. This can also serve up reality checks regarding your romantic life. Have you been putting someone on a pedestal? Have you been prioritizing your desire and pleasure in authentic ways? It’s time to throw away the rose-colored glasses.

Conversations feel profound and healing when Jupiter stations retrograde in Leo, also on December 12. You might learn news that changes your perspective or opens you up to new possibilities. This new information might take some time to integrate. This energy is also wonderful for going back to school, picking up a writing project, or booking some travel. You will learn more and more through lived experience over the coming months.

The Sun enters Capricorn and the Winter Solstice takes place on December 21, encouraging you to check in with your current priorities. How are you expending your time, energy, and resources? You might gain more attention for your talents over the coming weeks and with the extra attention comes extra pressure. This can also help you refine and reflect on who and what you’re focused on in your life. Does it feel truly meaningful and fulfilling?

A Full Moon in Cancer on December 23 puts you in touch with some emotions you thought you already sorted through. This can drudge up the past, which can feel jarring and overwhelming. This can help you confront and integrate some of your shadow side by feeling, processing, and being accountable in new ways. There might also be conversations around shared resources and finances, or reciprocity in certain relationships, contracts, or obligations in your life. If you feel underappreciated, now is the time to speak up.

The month wraps up with Mercury moving into Capricorn on December 25, helping you make important financial decisions. You might start applying for jobs or feel ready to set firmer boundaries at work or ask for a raise. You might also want to invest in yourself in some way—whether it’s buying a new coffee machine, booking a massage, or finding more time to thrift on the weekends. You know you can’t take your money with you when you leave this place so you might as well enjoy it.
